# MYSTIC
## HARMONY,
### OR THE AGREEMENT OF THE PHILOSOPHERS
->Chemical <note>Refers to alchemy, which during this period was frequently termed "the chemical art" or "chemical philosophy."</note>, with Scholia <note>Explanatory notes or commentaries.</note> on the most difficult passages of the Authors cited therein, whose names are found on the following pages.<-

->The whole work by Mr.<gloss>Sieur</gloss> L'AGNEAU of Aix-en-Provence, Counselor and Physician in Ordinary to the King.<-

->Translated by Mr.<gloss>Sieur</gloss> VEILLUTIL.<-

He who recognizes the consensus and agreement of the Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to the "Adepts" or masters of alchemy.</note> treating this subject, enjoys an admirable contentment, for many are led rather by blind opinion than by the study of the truth.

> For it is shameful to make a business of difficult trifles,
> And the labor spent on foolish things is vain. <note>original: "Turpe enim difficiles habere nugas, Et vanus labor est ineptiarum." — Martial, *Epigrams* 2.86.</note>

<meta>This page serves as the translator's preface, establishing a tone of intellectual honesty and defiance against the fraudulent alchemists and "puffers" of the era.</meta>

# THE TRANSLATOR
## The Commentator <note>Original: "Scholiaste." A commentator who provides explanatory notes or "scholia" on a text.</note> to his friend, wishes all prosperity.

I do not place myself under your wings, nor do I call upon you to take up the shield for defense, nor the sword for offense; crows and dogs are permitted to croak and bark. The <term>blowers</term> <note>From the French "souffleurs," a derogatory term for amateur or fraudulent alchemists who spent their time blowing on bellows to heat their furnaces without understanding the science.</note>, charcoal-burners, deceivers, charlatans, sellers of smoke <note>A common idiom for those who sell empty promises or fraudulent secrets.</note>, sellers of recipes, of white and red powders <note>Likely referring to cosmetic pigments or perhaps the "white" and "red" stages of the alchemical Great Work.</note>, hawks <note>A "tiercelet" is a male hawk, used here to describe predatory swindlers or intermediaries.</note>, usurers of one hundred percent and fifty percent, extractors of mercury from metals, forgers of various vessels and furnaces, builders of gallows, scaffolds, and similar infamous places where these scoundrels entangle themselves after having deceived themselves and many others—they may spout off to their heart’s content. "Great is Diana of the Ephesians!" <note>A reference to the Book of Acts 19:28, signifying a crowd blindly defending their own profitable superstitions or crafts.</note> This book will describe for us, reveal to us, and take away from us...

<summary>In this preface, the translator (acting as a commentator) addresses a friend, distancing himself from the fraudulent "blowers" and swindlers who populate the fringes of alchemical practice. He positions the following text as a revelation of truth that will expose these charlatans.</summary>

<keywords>translator's preface, blowers, alchemical fraud, charlatans, Diana of the Ephesians, mercury extraction</keywords>

...our <term>livelihood</term> <note>Original: "gagne-pain."</note>, and will show that we shall bring forth only a wretched mouse <note>A reference to the Roman poet Horace’s phrase "Parturient montes, nascetur ridiculus mus"—a satire on those who promise great things but deliver nothing.</note>; certainly if I could, I would either reform them all and in doing so make great savings, or being unable to succeed, I would have them dry in the Sun by day, and refresh in the Moon.

Now, Sir my friend, the ignorance of many causes their stupidity, and their evil soul precipitates them into the <term>labyrinth</term>, from there into <term>despair</term>, from there into the sea of anguish, and finally into the <term>abyss</term>. Your inclination toward this study and desire—the highest and most sublime that exists in the world and the most assured, and which requires most the blessing of God after <term>Holy Scripture</term>, in order to discern the true from the false, and the right from the sense which is contrary to it—has nudged me and, as it were, constrained me to put this piece into your hands. This is to show you the true and salutary path, and to make you see and recognize the steps of those false priests who, passing through the hidden door, ate the meats and drinks set before that great <term>Babylonian Bel</term> <note>A reference to the story of "Bel and the Dragon" from the biblical Apocrypha, where the prophet Daniel exposes the fraud of priests who entered through a secret door to eat food offerings left for an idol.</note>.

Consider, Sir my friend, the detours, the <term>mazes</term> <note>Original: "dedales," referring to the mythical Labyrinth of Daedalus.</note>, and the variety <meta>The OCR ends with the word "varieté" split across pages; the catchword "rieté" completes it.</meta> of paths bordered and strewn

<summary>The author warns his friend against the intellectual and spiritual dangers of fraudulent alchemy, comparing the deception of "puffers" to the biblical story of the priests of Bel. He offers this text as a guide to the "true and salutary path" of knowledge.</summary>

...of empty purses, of furnaces, of earthenware vessels, of metals, and of glass. Read again the variety and great number of <term>recipes</term> <note>Original: "receptes." In this context, these are alchemical formulas or "prescriptions" for the Great Work.</note>, the varieties of operations, and the variety and quantity of materials drawn from animals, vegetables, and <term>minerals</term>; and then cast your eyes (accompanied by an understanding illuminated by the true **Sun from above**) upon this piece that I present to you as a sacred gift. You will see that it agrees with all those ignorant men and their works about as much as heaven agrees with a toad <note>A common early modern expression used to illustrate an absolute and irreconcilable difference between the sublime and the base.</note>.

Truth is one, without variation, and seeks no hiding place; she asks for neither bravado nor <term>paint</term> <note>Original: "fard," meaning artificial makeup or deceptive adornment.</note>, being the true daughter of heaven. On the contrary, Falsehood—the mother of error—seeks out bravado, subtlety, abuses, and finally hiding places, from where, blinded, she plunges to a shameful death. 

Since you know all these things and have been disillusioned by a special grace from above, try to set your friends right by the reading of good authors. Although these <term>authors</term> may seem to vary in their words, they are not actually inconsistent, as you can now see; and if you judge, by the maturity of your judgment, to be

<summary>The author distinguishes the simplicity and singular nature of Truth from the complex, deceptive "mazes" of fraudulent alchemists. He encourages his friend to use this "sacred gift" of knowledge to guide others back to the study of genuine philosophical authors.</summary>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's introductory exhortation to his friend, transitioning from the critique of false alchemy on the previous page to a final instruction on the true path of natural philosophy and a closing blessing.</meta>

necessary to tear them from the claws of error; show them the most suitable order, always keeping to yourself the **two points**, which it is not permitted to declare to everyone, and without which no living soul can see the desired end <note>The "desired end" refers to the successful completion of the Great Work, the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. It will be enough, it seems to me, to lead them out of error and bring them back to prayer to God and to meditation on the works of nature, especially that by which the <term>animal</term> <note>In alchemical philosophy, "animal" often refers to the "living" or "animated" quality of matter, rather than just a biological creature.</note> is engendered, nourished in the <term>womb</term> <note>Original: "marrice." This term (matrix) refers to both the biological womb and the alchemical vessel where the "seed" of the metal is gestated.</note>, brought forth from it, and fed and raised. Afterward, each thing engenders its like, not by taking that from which the thing is engendered, but that which is produced from it. Live content and sober in your speech, and may God grant you the grace to see the end of your enterprise, and after a long and happy life, the joy and possession of His heavenly Kingdom, through the intercession of His Son, our sole and unique Redeemer, mediator, and intercessor. Amen.

<summary>The author concludes by advising his friend to keep the core secrets of the work private while guiding others toward a more pious and naturalistic approach to alchemy. He emphasizes the principle of "like engendering like" and offers a final religious blessing for the success of the recipient's spiritual and physical labors.</summary>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the author's private correspondence to a formal address to the general reader. It establishes the author's intent to provide a concise, honest guide to alchemy while warning against the many frauds operating in the field.</meta>

# TO THE READER.

If you are as much a friend of truth as I am an enemy of falsehood, you will be a diligent student of the good authors who treat our Philosophy, the goal of which is to possess the stone commonly called **philosophical** <note>The "Philosopher’s Stone," the legendary substance sought by alchemists to transmute base metals into gold and achieve physical and spiritual perfection.</note>. You must flee from the company and conversation of all charlatans, wanderers, <term>blowers</term> <note>Original: "souffleurs." A derogatory term for "puffers"—amateurs who focused solely on the physical heat of the furnace and bellows without understanding the underlying theory.</note>, makers and sellers of recipes, extractors of <term>quicksilver</term> <note>Original: "argent vif," meaning mercury.</note>, congealers, fixers, dyers, <term>fleecers</term> <note>Original: "tire-poils," literally "hair-pullers," a term for swindlers who fleece the gullible.</note>, and similar deceivers and forgers. By duping those who are too credulous, they lead them to nothing but the noose, shame, or a miserable life. If you believe me, you will do well; otherwise, regret shall follow you.

Now, through the seventeen chapters that follow (through which I could have produced a massive volume, but chose not to in order to avoid overwhelming you), you will learn from the First: that despite the diversity of names, the <term>matter</term> <note>The "Prima Materia" or First Matter. Alchemical texts often used hundreds of different names (like "Green Lion" or "Adamic Earth") to describe a single starting substance, confusing the uninitiated.</note> is not diverse, and that it is named either for its accidental or its essential form. By the Se-

<summary>In this preface, the author warns the reader to avoid fraudulent alchemists and "puffers," promising instead a concise seventeen-chapter guide to the true "matter" of the Philosopher's Stone.</summary>
<keywords>Philosophy, philosopher's stone, charlatans, blowers, quicksilver, mercury, prima materia, alchemy, swindlers</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory summary of the book's seventeen chapters. Having established in the first chapter that the "First Matter" is essentially one substance despite its many names, the author now outlines chapters two through nine, which detail the specific stages and requirements of the alchemical process.</meta>

second chapter, you will learn that there is only one single path and one single means to obtain this **stone** or **medicine** <note>Alchemists often referred to the Philosopher's Stone as a "medicine" because it was believed to "cure" base metals of their impurities and act as a universal elixir for human health.</note>, no matter how much those noisy, ignorant <term>blowers</term> <note>Original: "souffleurs." A derogatory term for "puffers"—unskilled practitioners who obsessed over the heat of the bellows and furnace without understanding alchemical theory.</note> bark and babble; and 
<margin>3</margin>through the third, you will have the order and the number, the materials, and the names of those things which you need to work with to produce for yourself the <term>matter</term>, without which nothing in this work can be done; and how this "one" must be withdrawn will
<margin>4</margin>be taught to you in the fourth chapter. And since there is something superfluous in this withdrawn matter, the 
<margin>5</margin>fifth chapter teaches the means 
<margin>6</margin>to remedy it. Through the sixth, you will learn that all the operations mentioned by all the authors can be reduced to five, which are <term>composition</term>, <term>digestion</term>, <term>extraction</term>, <term>nutrition</term>, and <term>fixation</term> <note>These represent the chemical and symbolic stages of transforming the matter, from mixing (composition) and slow heating (digestion) to making the substance resistant to fire (fixation).</note>. 
<margin>7</margin>Through the seventh, you will learn that that which is extracted—which is a black powder, or sometimes the color of brick, fine to the touch, oily, and somewhat stinking and bitter—must be nourished, and with 
<margin>8</margin>what and how. The eighth will teach you what <term>fire</term> <note>In alchemy, "fire" could refer to physical heat, but also to "secret fire"—a chemical agent or internal energy that drives the transformation.</note> is necessary for you. The 
<margin>9</margin>ninth, what <term>vessel</term> <note>The "vessel" is the alchemical container, often called the "Philosophical Egg," which had to be carefully shaped and sealed to maintain the proper environment for the "Great Work."</note> you must have.

<summary>The author outlines chapters two through nine of the treatise, defining the singular path to the Philosopher's Stone and listing the five essential alchemical operations: composition, digestion, extraction, nutrition, and fixation.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the author's introductory preface. The opening fragment "ment" completes a word from the previous page—likely "clairement" (clearly) or "entièrement" (entirely)—finishing the thought that the work can be understood through the following text. The author transitions from the summary of chapters to a final spiritual exhortation, emphasizing that success in alchemy is a divine gift that must be used for charitable ends.</meta>

...ly, for there is nothing hidden, nor anything more to be desired; it is true that the **extraction** and **dissolution**, or the **nourishment of the black** <note>The "nourishment of the black" refers to the *nigredo* stage, the first phase of the alchemical Great Work, where the matter is decomposed and purified.</note> are two operations which require visual observation of the process. However, if you read this entire discourse attentively, and reread it with meditation, you will be able to achieve it; these two points are easy to do for one who understands them, yet very difficult, even for the most experienced. 

And if God grants you the grace to find them, the rest is as nothing, so to speak. Praise Him then and thank Him, and let your joy be internal; whether in working or in enjoying your harvest, be secretive, and use it for the honor and praise of Him who will have opened your eyes and sharpened your understanding, and for the consolation of the poor members of **Jesus Christ**, who must judge us all at His **second coming**, which shall occur, however long it may be delayed, at the time determined since the foundation of the world.

This, Reader, is what I wanted to tell you.

<vocab>extraction, dissolution, nourishment of the black, meditation, Jesus Christ, second coming</vocab>

<summary>The author concludes the preface by explaining that while the initial stages of the alchemical process are difficult and require careful observation, they can be mastered through study and divine grace. He charges the successful alchemist to remain secretive and use their wealth to help the poor in anticipation of the Second Coming.</summary>
<keywords>nigredo, alchemical operations, divine grace, secrecy, charity, eschatology, Second Coming</keywords>

One should have a great abundance of books, and let no one persuade himself that a single book can be found which truly describes the whole art in a continuous discourse, as many foolish people believe. *Theobald van Hoghelande, page 11.*

You should not waste your goods, because (if you are not ignorant of the principles of the art) you will reach the completion of the mastery at a low price. *Geber, Book 1, Chapter 7.* <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, an 8th-century polymath and foundational figure in alchemy.</note>

Hold this firmly: that for all the chapters of this most precious art, the cost of the medicines themselves does not exceed fifty silver pieces, counting from the first operation. *Arnaldus de Villa Nova, Book 2, Chapter 7 of the Rosary.* <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311) was a Catalan physician and reputed alchemist.</note>

It is necessary to bring many arguments against firmly held opinions if one wishes to lead a listener away from a previous sentiment and convert them, so that they may have faith in one’s words. Indeed, I would not have produced these [arguments] to confirm my discourse unless there were many men who opposed my opinion. *Hippocrates, Book 4 on Diseases, Section 1.*

Note that in the book of the antiquities of Paris, on page eleven, it is remarked that in the Reliquary of Saint Genevieve there are nine-score thirteen <note>193</note> and a half marks of silver at forty-five Parisian sous per mark, and eight and a half marks of gold at sixteen francs per mark; this was in the year one thousand two hundred forty-two.

And that during the reign of Louis XI in 1475, the gold *écus* <note>crowns</note> were only worth 24 Parisian sous and 6 deniers; let him who has ears and judgment understand and judge.

<summary>This page concludes an index of alchemical authorities and provides citations emphasizing that the "Art" requires many books rather than one, is relatively inexpensive to perform, and includes historical currency comparisons for Paris.</summary>

And in conclusion, this matter changed its name as it changed its nature, until, having acquired its perfection, it became fixed. *Isaac Holland, Book 1, Chapter 126, On Mineral Operations.* <note>Isaac Hollandus was a celebrated, though perhaps legendary, 15th-century Dutch alchemist whose works were highly influential in the 17th century.</note>

### Scholium or Brief Exposition.

<margin>
The understanding of this science is <term>Kabbalistic</term> <note>original: "Caballestique". In alchemy, this refers to the use of secret, symbolic language and mystical interpretation to hide truths from the "unworthy."</note>; let him who has sense believe it; this is why authors say one must have a deep judgment to understand their writings, which have one thing on the surface and another beneath, indeed, one within the marrow.
</margin>

Galen <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the famous Greek physician whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> remarks that while the <term>Sophists</term> <note>Here referring to those who argue over definitions and logic rather than observing nature or performing practical work.</note> debate the names of things, time is lost, and ignorance of what is sought remains; this is the reason why he wished that things could be communicated and understood without names, thereby removing the opportunity for Sophists and the contentious (who stop only at the surface of words) to incessantly pull the truth into doubtful controversies, which in the end produce nothing but irresolution and uncertainty. For there is nothing that tangles and obscures knowledge more than these vain and useless disputes over names, which have pushed most learned people into very tangled labyrinths of error. Now, several writings have been made in such a way that in some of them, one finds three types of understanding, or senses. The first of which, com—

# CHAPTER I.

...as the skin and the bark, is known and understood by everyone, and is called **Literal**. The second is **Moral** or **Allegorical**, and is like the flesh covered by the skin, yet perceivable to one who looks within; and the **Anagogical** <term>Anagogical</term> <note>From the Greek *anagoge*, meaning "leading up." This is the highest level of interpretation, looking toward spiritual, mystical, or heavenly realities.</note> and **Divine** sense is like the most hidden bones, covered by flesh and skin, and full of marrow. This sort and manner of writing has been used in all times by the most learned, and not only in writing, but even in the manner of speaking; and without stopping to look for examples, all of Holy Scripture is full of it, and our Lord Jesus Christ, having spoken obscurely to the people, said to his Disciples: "it is to you that it belongs to understand the mystery of the Kingdom of Heaven." <note>A reference to Matthew 13:11, where Christ explains why he speaks in parables to the crowds but plainly to his inner circle.</note>

After the Holy Scripture contained in the Canonical books of the Old and New Testament, there is no other text under which, to obtain the secret, one must stretch the mind more than in that where the purification of metals is described, and which is commonly called the **Philosophers’ Stone**; as witnessed by the text above, which we shall begin to examine, and we shall learn what we do not know, either through another who shows us the way and the path, whether by word or by sign; or by ourselves alone, meditating or reasoning without any master; and both are given by Nature and aided by art and method; and one must not be astonished if few people profit from the reading of books, or even if many reject them, since many reject the reading of

...Holy Scripture, because, they say, many abuse it, just as they do the sound of bells <note>A common period proverb suggesting that people hear whatever they want to hear in ambiguous sounds; the "fool" hears his own thoughts in the tolling of the bell.</note>; many even among them preferring to dispute obstinately—even about that which does not fall under the senses or reason because it is so simple—rather than to surrender and yield to the truth.

The **Sages**: this word is not intended to refer to all those who are deemed wise, but to those who, through study and the conversations they have had with many learned men, have acquired the science and knowledge of many great and admirable things. Concerning them, George Venetus <note>Francesco Zorzi (1466–1540), a Venetian Franciscan friar and Christian Kabbalist. It was believed that health depended on balancing these bodily fluids.</note>; what is proper to temper the heart, the liver, the stomach, and such other parts. And in order to do this better, they added Astronomy to it, believing that the infirmities and the natures of herbs, roots, and other medicinal things could only be judged by the Planets and Stars with which they correspond.

Now those whom the Persians called **Magi**, the Egyptians called **Priests**, the Indians **Gymnosophists** <term>Gymnosophists</term> <note>From the Greek for "naked philosophers," referring to the ancient Indian ascetics known for their wisdom and renunciation of worldly goods.</note>, the Gauls **Druids**, and the Greeks *Sophos* <note>original: "Sophos," Greek for "wise."</note>, whom the Latins call *Sapiens* <note>original: "Sapiens," Latin for "wise."</note>, and the French *Sages*. It is therefore of these Sages that our Author speaks here; who, writing of a science the highest (after the knowledge of God) that can exist—since he who has acquired it has nothing more to desire in this world, treating as worthless <note>The original French "faisant litière" literally means "making straw bedding of," implying that material riches are as common and lowly as stable straw compared to this wisdom.</note> all the riches therein—writes with such restraint— <meta>catchword: "rete-", likely continuing as "retenue" (restraint or reserve) on the following page.</meta>

### CHAPTER I.

...plainly <note>original: "nuë," literally naked. In this context, it means describing the matter so clearly that it entices the wise while confusing the ignorant.</note> that he wishes to entice the truly learned and wise to the search for it, and to send the asses back to the thistles <note>A common alchemical metaphor: the "asses" (ignorant seekers) are left with the prickly, worthless literal meanings, while the "wise" find the true nourishment.</note>; therefore, these sages have given several names to the <term>Stone</term> <note>The "Philosophers' Stone," the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and providing an elixir of life.</note>.

Our Author makes use of this word **Stone**, as it is the most common and received by all the other Sages, who call "Stone" everything that does not vanish in the fire: now our Stone being completed, cannot be altered in any way in the world, by anything whatsoever, whether simple or compound; but it seems, and there is some appearance, that he does not understand this word "Stone" by this perfection, since he says:

After having drawn the matter from the Stone, and having rendered it subtle and spirituous <note>In alchemy, "spirituous" refers to a substance that has been refined into a volatile, vapor-like, or highly active state.</note>, this matter is called **vile**. For if it is perfect, it follows that it no longer suffers increase or decrease; if then from this perfect Stone one drew the matter which he intends, it would follow <margin>to the point</margin> that it would not be perfect. Let us come then to the point: the Sages thus make a composition of two raw and clean substances with their own pure and clean agent, in the appropriate weight, and of which it will be spoken hereafter, God willing; which becomes so hard in a few hours that it is impossible to break it without a hammer or other hard and solid thing. Now from this Mass, to which our Author gives the name of **Stone** because of this hardness, is drawn—by the order known only to the Sages and those learned in this science—a subtle matter, which is in an impalpable and volatile powder upon the fire, which he calls **spirituous**, which is the foundation of the **Art** <note>The "Art" is a standard term for Alchemy or the "Great Work."</note>, and without which it is...

<summary>This page explains that alchemists use the term "Stone" for any substance that resists fire, but specifically describes a hard mass created from two raw substances. From this hard "Stone," a volatile, "spirituous" powder is extracted, which serves as the true foundation of the alchemical Art.</summary>

### CHAPTER I.

...naked truth, such that he wishes to entice the truly learned and wise to the search for it, and to send the asses back to the thistles <note>A common alchemical idiom: the "asses" are the ignorant or greedy who seek only gold, while "thistles" are the worthless or difficult distractions they deserve.</note>; therefore these Sages have given several names to the <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the legendary goal of alchemy, believed to transform base metals into gold and provide the Elixir of Life.</note>.

Our Author makes use of this word "Stone" because it is the most common and accepted by all the other Sages, who call a "Stone" anything that does not vanish in the fire. Now, our Stone, once completed, cannot be altered in any way in the world by anything whatsoever, whether simple or compound; but it seems, and there is some appearance of it, that he does not mean this word "Stone" to refer to this final perfection, since he says:

</margin>

After having extracted the matter of the Stone, and having made it subtle and spiritualized, this matter is called "vile." <note>original: "matiere vile." Alchemists often claimed the "First Matter" was a common, cheap, or "vile" substance that the ignorant overlook, such as earth or even waste.</note> For if it is perfect, it follows that it no longer suffers increase or decrease; if, then, one extracted the matter he intends from this perfect Stone, it would follow that it was not yet perfect. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the alchemical "Stone" and the specific terminology used by "Sages." ->### CHEMICAL HARMONY<-

...impossible to find anything good to complete and bring the metals called imperfect <note>Alchemists believed metals like lead and tin were "immature" or "imperfect" versions of gold and silver, which could be "ripened" through the alchemical art.</note> to the degree of silver or gold; this matter will therefore be without a proper name in this Author, but we will soon find others who will give it names, for some will call it *Sulphur*, others *Mercury*, others *double Mercury*, others *animated Mercury*, others *permanent Water*, and others by other names, of which we will give some clarification in the proper place. Now, this subtle and spiritualized matter named "vile"—that is to say, of little value or of nothing—(it must be noted that he does not simply say it *is* vile, but it is *named* vile, a way of speaking considered by few people) having been sublimated, they have named it "Serpent" and "venomous beast." 

This sublimation <term>sublimation: in alchemy, this is not just the transition from solid to gas, but a process of purifying a substance and "raising" it to a spiritual or more potent state</term> of which this Author speaks is not the common sublimation used by vulgar Chemists <note>Often called "souffleurs" (bellows-blowers), these were practitioners who sought only to make gold through literal, physical chemistry without understanding the philosophical or spiritual underpinnings.</note>, but it is an order and means by which this subtle, spiritualized, and so-called vile matter is made more excellent through the whiteness or redness <note>The "White" and "Red" works (albedo and rubedo) are the final stages of the Great Work, resulting in the stones for silver and gold, respectively.</note> acquired for it. 

But why was it named, when it is sublimated, *Serpent and venomous beast*? It is because just as the serpent glides insensibly, so too this vile matter enters and penetrates its extractor <note>The "extractor" likely refers to the base substance or "body" from which the essence is being drawn, or the metal being acted upon.</note>, and having penetrated and entered into it, reduces it to its own substance, so much so that it takes away its first being, and annihilates it such that it is no longer, and will never again be what it was, whatever artifice one applies to it; and for this reason it is called a *venomous beast*: for it is the nature of such venomous animals to kill that which, having life, it...

<summary>This page explains the diverse names given to the "First Matter" of alchemy (such as Sulphur or Mercury) and describes its transformative power. It emphasizes that this "vile" matter, once purified through sublimation, acts like a "venomous beast" by completely consuming and altering the substance it touches.</summary>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of the "vile matter" or "Serpent" introduced on the previous page. It details how this substance is transformed through the stages of calcination (becoming Salt), dissolution (becoming Water), and reduction (becoming Oil), emphasizing that these names are symbolic blinds to hide the truth from the "ignorant."</meta>

# CHAPTER I.

<meta>ink stains on right margin and bottom; catchword "ferme-" visible at bottom right; bleed-through from reverse page</meta>

is contrary.

This subtle, spiritual matter named "vile," having been sublimated and named *Serpent*, must be calcined <term>calcination: the process of heating a substance to a high temperature to reduce it to a powder; in alchemy, this is often seen as the "death" of the material's old form</term>—that is to say, made white—by the method we will describe in its proper place; and then it is named *salt*, not because it is salty, but because it is stable, firm, and fixed, and because it can serve as an ornament to metals inferior to silver, just as salt gives grace and taste to the substances to which it is applied.

This *dissolved* matter—that is to say, extended far and wide in both quantity and quality—is named *Water* and *found everywhere*. I well know that some understand this "found everywhere" to be said because of the four Elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water—the fundamental building blocks of the universe in pre-modern science.</note>, which I do not contradict, but I say that this way of speaking, like many others, is used to hide the secret from the ignorant, some of whom believe it to be dew-water, urine, saltpeter, *aqua fortis* <note>original: "eau forte," a strong nitric acid used to dissolve metals</note>, *aqua regia* <note>original: "eau royale," a mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold</note>, and other waters that wet everything they are placed upon. Let it be noted, then, for the second time, that our Author does not simply say it *is* Water, but it is *named* Water and *found everywhere*. This matter, then, after being calcined, must be reduced into *oil*, and then it is called a *viscous thing* and *found in all places for sale*. One must not believe that it is a flowing, greasy, and burning oil; rather, after this matter is whitened and named Water, it is fit for whitening, but it must be made capable of remaining upon the matter onto which it will be cast, just as oil clings firmly to the piece upon which it has fallen.

->A iiij<-

<meta>This page continues the technical and symbolic explanation of the stages of the alchemical Great Work. While the previous page discussed the "Serpent," "Salt," and "Water," this page focuses on the properties of the "Oil," the process of "Congealing" (solidification), and the critical transition from the white stage (the albedo) to the red stage (the rubedo).</meta>

->### HARMONIE CHYMIQVE<-

And thus, having such a property, it is called viscous or sticky, but more difficult (indeed impossible) to be removed than oils and glues; and no one can erase what it has caused, other than the Artist <term>Artist: a traditional term for the alchemist or practitioner of the Hermetic arts</term> himself. Now, this oily and viscous matter is found for sale everywhere, just like the water mentioned above.

This operation of congealing does not follow directly after the oil, for the oil is no longer suitable for it; rather, it is like the first operation. For we call "congealed" <margin>fluid</margin> that which, having previously been fluid like water or oil, thickens and freezes through great cold; likewise, the agent in this matter, mixed with its patient <note>In alchemy, the "agent" is the active, masculine principle (often Sulfur), and the "patient" is the passive, feminine principle (often Mercury) that receives the action.</note>, becomes hard, and only softens by fire. And then this matter which was flowing, having been rendered hard, is called <unclear>earth</unclear>: if the poor and rich have it, it must be understood as being found everywhere.

Having whitened it is the same thing as having calcined it; I am well aware that it can be whitened and blackened many times, but this reiteration is not intended here. These names, *virgin’s milk* <note>original: "lait virginal," a symbolic name for the white, mercurial liquid that appears during the work</note>, and *of all whiteness*, sufficiently mark this first whiteness before the oil, inasmuch as being white, it is calcined, then is rendered fit for dyeing in fixed white—so much so that this white would not be fixed if it could still be whitened or faded. Consequently, it could not be raised to redness, as he adds next, saying: having raised it to redness, it is called fire, and by the name, *of all redness*. Read and meditate, then, attentively; our matter is drawn from two

perfect bodies, pure and clean, to which fire, however violent it may be, nor any other thing simple or compound, can add or diminish anything; by means of that from which they had their beginning, the three (by means of a fourth, known and put to use by everyone) yield a subtle, impalpable matter (yet which stains <note>The transcription says "fallit," likely a variation of "salit" (stains/dirties), describing how the matter leaves a mark despite being "subtle."</note> the fingers of him who touches it) and it is part volatile, part fixed, as you will see. For if one puts it in a crucible, the volatile part evaporates and the fixed part varnishes or vitrifies the crucible. I will speak of other signs in the proper place; this matter—partly subtle and spirituous, and partly earthy—is named in many and various ways, and does not hold the nature of any of its components, from which it degenerates if it is not governed as it should be. That is to say, it is of no worth, no more than the seed come from a healthy and strong man will be worth anything if it is not cast and darted into its proper place. But if our matter is governed by good order, it will overcome the perfect bodies from which it emerged, without however there being any alteration in said bodies, except for some concealment <term>palliation: a temporary masking or covering of the true state of the metals during the reaction</term> during their action. This matter, then, must be separated once it has come forth or been born from the whole, and being separated, it must be nourished by its first milk, which it converts into its own substance, making this milk subtle and spirituous like itself; and it will continue to be nourished by it until its swarthy complexion is whitened. # HARMONY OF CHEMISTRY

—ly to those who understand) is said to be named *brass* <note>original: "vrain," an archaic spelling or printer's error for "l'airain" (brass).</note>, for brass does not emerge as such from the earth: but is a composite, and being a composite it does not retain the name of any of its components, but a specific one. Likewise, this matter retains the name of its attribute, and the name *gold* is given to it because of its excellence, and those of *earth* and *Magnesia*—not because of its region which is in Macedonia adjacent to Thessaly named Magnesia, nor because of the city called Magnesia in Ionia near the Maeander, distant about sixteen thousand paces from Ephesus, nor from that species of <term>Marcasite: a historical term for various glittering minerals, often iron sulfides</term> named by some Magnesia and by others <term>Pyrites: minerals like "fool's gold" that produce sparks when struck</term>. Rather, it is named, as it appears, from the name of *Magnes*, or the magnet, for just as the magnet attracts the crudest metal to itself, so too does this science attract to itself the coarsest among men who—though they are desirous—are ignorant of the being, the beginning, the middle, and the end of *the entire compound*, which is the *true Salamander*. 

This is not to say that it is the animal so named, for this animal (nor any other, though some say the contrary) is not conceived, nourished, and perfected in the fire; rather, this amalgam, when placed in a suitable fire, engenders therein a "son" who, through the continuation of that fire, is nourished and perfected. This is not to say that the fire by itself does all this without the addition of "drink" and "solid meat," as some empty-headed people believe; instead, these solid drinks and meats, added at the proper time, are helped by the fire to act and to be acted upon <note>original: "agir & patir." In alchemical philosophy, this refers to the relationship between the "agent" (the active force) and the "patient" (the substance being transformed).</note>, just as by the natural heat in the bodies of animals, meats and drinks are helped, some to act and others to be acted upon; and I do not see—

<summary>The author explains that alchemical terms like "brass" and "Magnesia" are metaphorical titles for a composite substance that acts like a magnet. He clarifies that the "Salamander" is not a literal animal but a chemical amalgam that "feeds" on additions and is perfected by the heat of the fire, much like digestion.</summary>

# CHAPTER I.

the Stone <insert>as</insert> a subtle and spirituous matter, but our author says straight away that *the Philosophers’ Stone is one*, not that it is a "Stone" in this beginning—that is to say, fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixed" refers to a substance that is stable and does not evaporate or change when exposed to heat.</note> against every test—but he looks toward its end. This is why, in describing the degrees through which this Stone or matter passes, he says that it is *watery*, because it is moist; indeed, in making the amalgam <note>An "amalgam" is a mixture of mercury with another metal; here it refers to the initial merging of alchemical ingredients.</note> it is fluid almost like water. It is also *airy* or subtle like the air, and penetrating like it; *fiery* because it dries out the superfluous humidity of metals that are too soft, and consumes that which is not and cannot be made fixed; *Earthy* because of its weight; *phlegmatic* for its moisture and whiteness; *Choleric* by its heat and yellowness; *Melancholic* by its initial blackness and dryness; *sulfurous* by its property of separating the combustible from the non-combustible; *Quicksilver* <note>original: "Argent vif"; another name for mercury, considered by alchemists to be the "mother" of all metals.</note> because it returns its own blood into its own nature, just as quicksilver does to metals. We shall speak of these superfluities hereafter in the fifth chapter, but meanwhile it must be noted that this author asserts what he says to be true, and that this is done by means of our fire—which is not understood as the common fire, clear, bright, and heating, but ours, he says, which is nothing other than what he calls the unique Stone; that is to say, a homogeneous substance that cannot be separated into various parts different from one another. For we may also say that by wateriness and phlegm they signify the white color, and by the airy and the choleric, the yellow color, which is the end of the whiteness and the beginning of the redness.

**T**his author names this subtle, spirituous matter emerging from bodies as a *stone* and a *grain of wheat* by way of similarity (not of identity); for certainly, if this Stone is not governed as the Sages have taught, it remains useless and will produce nothing. to give knowledge of it; but that which has no name, and which one wants to make known or understood, requires either a name or a description of its being and its effect. This is why this Author says that because this matter, or composition of three, has no proper name, several are attributed to it, such as *chaos* or *confused mass*, since this amalgam is neither gold, nor silver, nor mercury, but all three; and also the *origin of the world*, because from it the four elements, or four colors, emerge. To *deceive the ignorant*, as he says, which can be compared to what our Lord Jesus Christ said: that one must not sow pearls before swine. It is also like what is recorded in **Esdras** <note>Referring to the apocryphal book of 2 Esdras (or 4 Ezra), popular in esoteric circles for its visions.</note>, where the Angel **Uriel** <note>One of the archangels in Judeo-Christian tradition, often associated with divine wisdom and the interpretation of visions.</note> tells him to publish one part of the books he dictated for the common people, but the other part, which was the smallest, for the wise and understanding. The bronze <term>airain: while usually meaning bronze or brass, in alchemy this represents the crude, base state of the metal before purification</term> at the beginning of its cooking becomes water, <gloss>and</gloss> always thickens while cooking, until the stone is more excellent than all metals, which is the reason why it is called the *Philosophers' Stone*. The same, page 142.

Ripley <note>George Ripley, the 15th-century English alchemist</note> follows the manner of speaking of Arnaldus <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova, a famous medieval physician and alchemist</note> and Hortulanus <note>A legendary alchemist, author of a influential commentary on the Emerald Tablet of Hermes</note>, saying *our stone*, but he adds that which we have need of for our work; these are not, therefore, several matters, but one, namely that in which the Sun and the Moon <note>In alchemy, "Sun" and "Moon" typically represent Gold and Silver.</note> have been reduced by means of Mercury; but let the Reader and the student in this arduous research be satisfied for once that our Sun, Moon, and Mercury are not common gold, silver, nor quicksilver <note>original: *argent vif*</note>. For these common ones are dead: but ours, although they have issued from them, are living, and of the manner of this extraction, I shall speak in the proper time; meanwhile, let us learn that this matter must not be reduced to *hyle* <note>original: *hyle* — a Greek term for the primordial, unformed substance of the universe</note> or the invisible and imperceptible to the outer senses, nor also to the *chaos* <note>original: *cahos*</note> composed of heterogeneous matters <note>Substances made of different, unmixed parts</note>, but rather into the more immediate matter, which is homogeneous <note>A substance that is uniform and consistent throughout</note>, to which various names are appropriated: (as has been said before, and shall be said again hereafter, if it please God) but usually with the addition of *our*, or *of the wise*, or *of the Philosophers*; as proof of which he says, *Our matter*, *our stone*, and not simply matter or stone, placing himself by this word *our* among the number of those who have known the composition of this admirable work of which he speaks, like the Master to the disciple, whom he warns has *infinite names*, and at which one must not stop entirely, seeing that by all of them only one and the same thing is meant. The *Bronze* <term>airain: while it usually means bronze or brass, in alchemy it refers to the crude, unrefined state of the metal</term> then, of which he names the whole matter or composition, is [begins]- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "ent-".</note>

# CHAPTER I.

...being made water: now this water is understood in three ways: either when the whole compound is liquid, or when it is an impalpable and black powder, or when it has acquired whiteness; in all of which it is partly volatile <term>volatile: a substance that easily evaporates or escapes when heated</term>, and partly vitrifying <term>vitrifying: the process of turning into glass, or becoming "fixed" so it no longer evaporates</term>. I say and affirm it to be volatile in the black, the white, the yellow, and the red stages, being unable to be fixed upon the fire, except by its mother in the white stage, and by its father in the red stage. <margin><insert>Read</insert></margin> For the yellow is the end of the white and the beginning of the red; this is why he adds that this brass <note>original: *airain* — a term often used in alchemy to describe the base matter as it begins to transform</note> *always thickens while cooking* through the intended order, which will raise it to a most eminent degree, but one more excellent than those "Most Eminents" <note>The author likely refers to high-ranking officials or cardinals, noting that the Stone’s "splendor" is superior because it can be shared without being lost.</note> who cannot communicate their splendor to others without diminishing their own. But this brass, by communicating its most eminent purple <note>The "purple" or "red" stage is the final, most perfect state of the alchemical work.</note> to its inferiors, makes itself even more desirable, more sought after, and more admirable in all things; so that, being at such a high degree of perfection from which it can never fall, it acquires the name **Philosophers' Stone**, or the Stone of the Wise. If one calls it *water* at that point, one speaks the truth (by its properties); if one says the opposite, one does not lie (by similarity), charitably warning the reader not to get stuck on the diversity of names, so as not to be deceived.

Now, he says, *when they are cooked wisely, they become one.* They are therefore several, at least three, of which two will never agree—since one is hot and dry and the other cold and moist—except by the means and mediation of a third, a friend to both, who can unite and bind them in such a way that they will forever be inseparably *one*.

# CHAPTER I.

...considering this word "metal," one will find that this word "metal" in Greek comes from the verb *metallo*, or *metalleuo* <note>original: *metallo*, *metallefuo*</note>, which means to dig, or to search, or according to some from *para ta meta ta alla ef-frisqueste* <note>original Greek: παρὰ τὰ μετὰ τὰ ἄλλα εὑρίσκεσθαι — meaning "because they are found one after another"</note>, which signifies that one rarely finds one vein of metal without finding another very close by. It is called **Soul**, insofar as just as all animals and vegetables live, grow, flourish, and multiply through the soul, likewise, once the physical stone <note>the Philosopher's Stone</note> is prepared and the soul is joined to it, it becomes beautiful and resplendent, nourishing itself and growing. It is called **Ointment**, because just as fats and ointments soften and make the things to which they are applied lubricated or slippery, similarly this oil softens the hard parts of the stone, smoothing the rough parts and making them flow. It is called **Sulfur**, because it acts upon the matter by congealing and fixing it into the form of sulfur. It is called **Light** by comparison to the soul, which illuminates the body. Finally, it is called **Alum** <note>original: *Alun* — a mineral salt used as a mordant in dyeing</note> by similarity to dyers, who use alum for the dyeing of their cloths. # CHAPTER I.

...unlike the hard and solid body, the elements of the chemists <note>original: *Chymiques* — meaning alchemists</note> are called composites, because they do not look for simple elements (from which they could not generate anything) but for composites, in which one quality always dominates over the three others joined to it. For instance, they call "water" that in which the qualities of water dominate—namely, coldness and moisture. The same author, chapter 2, page 21.

<margin>Vogelius <note>likely Ewaldus Vogelius, a 16th-century author of alchemical treatises</note></margin>

### Scholium <note>an explanatory note or commentary added to a text</note>.

Our author, having mentioned "oil," defines what it is to point out to students that this is not common oil—the kind that burns, flames, and provides light. However, regarding the manner in which this oil or silt <note>original: *limon* — often referring to the "primordial slime" or raw material of the Work</note> is made, and from what it is made, he does not speak at all. He only says that once it is made, it does not mix with the substances from which it was created; instead, it gathers upon its water or solvent <note>original: *menstrue* — from the Latin *menstruum*, a substance used to dissolve solids</note> that dissolved the matter. This matter, once separated and collected, is called "water" (of which much has already been said) and "leaven," because this <insert>blackness or oily silt converts fifty times its own weight of the solvent</insert> by means of which it was produced. Without this, every practitioner <note>original: *artiste* — literally "artist," the traditional name for an alchemist</note> will labor in vain, because without it the Mastery <note>original: *magistere* — the completed alchemical process or the Philosopher's Stone itself</note> can be neither started nor finished, whether at its beginning,

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion on the nature of alchemical "composites." It concludes the thought that the work has many names and begins a critique of misguided alchemists who misunderstand the nature of matter and dissolution. The catchword "degré" (degree) indicates the beginning of the next page's focus.</meta>

->**CHEMICAL HARMONY**<-

...middle, and end has a thousand names, explaining the reason for a portion of them, and leaving the other portion for the students to research.

Almost all researchers in this study strain <note>original: *alambiquent* — literally "to distill," but here used metaphorically to mean over-complicating or over-working the mind, a pun on the alchemist's alembic</note> their minds to distill, decompose, and reduce—or so they say—their materials into four simple elements, of which they claim everything is composed. Then, from such rejoined simple elements, they expect to perform miracles. But these "miracles" are the opposite of God, who from nothing—that is to say, from no visible or palpable matter—made everything that is both visible and palpable. Instead, these coarse seekers make nothing out of everything. In doing so, they fulfill the misunderstood saying of the **Philo-chemists** <note>original: *Philochymiques* — literally "lovers of alchemy," referring to the master philosophers of the craft</note> who command us to reduce the substance from which the necessary matter is drawn into its "first being." By this, they mean the nearest state and not a distant one, which is the **simple matter** that proceeds **from the dissolved body**. 

This dissolution, or annihilation, is very poorly understood by these coarse operators. This dissolution is the true **squaring of the circle** <note>a classic alchemical symbol for the union of opposites and the transformation of the physical (the square) into the spiritual (the circle)</note> reduced to the triangle, to the line, and to the indivisible point. This point contains as much in its indivisibility as did the line, the triangle, the quadrangle, and the circle, without the manual separation of any thing, but only through the attraction of new qualities, one after the other. This circle is a perfect thing to which neither nature nor man can add or subtract; only the one who is wise and learned in this science (I say science, for it is sought and desired for its own sake) knows how to lead it to a degree in which he will show more plainly...

<term>alambiquer</term> — To distill; used here to describe over-complicating the mind. 
<term>four elements</term> — Fire, Air, Water, and Earth; the traditional building blocks of matter.
<term>Philo-chemists</term> — Ancient and medieval masters of alchemical philosophy.
<term>simple matter</term> — The purified, essential substance of the Work.
<term>dissolved body</term> — Matter that has been broken down to its primary state.
<term>squaring of the circle</term> — A symbolic mathematical operation representing the completion of the Great Work.
<term>triangle</term> — Often representing the three principles: Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury.
<term>quadrangle</term> — Representing the four elements.
<term>attraction</term> — The process by which matter draws in new spiritual or physical qualities.

<summary>The author critiques amateur alchemists who over-complicate the process of reduction and argues that true dissolution is a spiritual-geometric perfection (squaring the circle) rather than a simple physical destruction of matter.</summary>
<keywords>dissolution, squaring the circle, simple matter, four elements, alchemical theory, spiritual geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "squaring of the circle" and the reduction of matter. The previous page ended with "Thi..." (This/This dissolution), which continues here as "its virtue." The wavy line in the margin highlights the section describing the physical transformations and colors of the alchemical matter.</meta>

### CHAPTER I.

its virtue; and from this degree, which is the fourth or **squaring** <note>original: *quadrature* — a reference to the "squaring of the circle," the alchemical process of making the spiritual (circle) physical (square)</note>, it descends to the third; from the third to the second, named the line; and from the second or line to the first or point, which is **indivisible** <note>the "indivisible point" refers to the ultimate reduction of matter to its simplest, non-spatial essence before it is rebuilt</note>. And from this indivisible point, it raises itself up, mounting to the seventh; from there to the lowest, and from the lowest back to the fourth, in which it is ignited in such a way that its interior and exterior color becomes like a dark red, yet dazzling and brilliant. *The Elements of the Chemists are therefore called composites*: for if they were not, that which has been said could not emerge from them. The variety of names for this **simple matter** has been given for two main reasons: the first is because of the changes that occur—being sometimes liquid, sometimes a little firmer, sometimes dry, sometimes a tree, sometimes a powder, sometimes greasy and floating, sometimes heavy and sinking to the bottom, sometimes **volatile**, sometimes frozen, sometimes **fixed**, sometimes nourishing itself on its own milk, sometimes on its white or red body, sometimes black, sometimes white, sometimes yellow, sometimes red. The other reason is to keep the "beasts at the thistles" <note>a metaphorical expression meaning to keep the foolish or unworthy occupied with prickles and surface-level difficulties</note>—those who wish to enjoy such a great good without any effort. The Gods, say the ancients, sell their treasures for the price of sweat, and true **knowledge** is not acquired while sleeping.

> *Venerable knowledge does not lie in a soft bed.* <note>original Latin: *Non iacet in molli veneranda fcientia lecto.*</note>
> *She herself comes, produced by constant labor.* <note>original Latin: *Ipfa, sed affiduo parta labore venit.*</note>

The dominance, then, of each of the aforementioned qualities is the cause of the diversity of names: for when this matter is liquid, it is called by the names of all liquid things; when it is hard, by the [names of all hard things]...

By the first text, which we may call a corollary, it is proven that the **Philochymists** <term>Philochymiques: lovers of alchemy or "chemical friends"</term> ordinarily use the word *our* almost everywhere. # CHEMICAL HARMONY

[Our] work is called **crude** <note>original: *crud*</note>, while the materials that compose it are still whole, and have not yet yielded their seed; but once it is yielded, it is named **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: *argent vif*, or living mercury</term> and **permanent water** for the same reason given in the first text; and **lead** for two reasons: one because of its color, and because it floats atop the **Sun**, the **Moon**, and **Mercury** <note>In alchemy, these names—Sun, Moon, and Mercury—represent Gold, Silver, and the metallic principle, respectively.</note>. He does not mean by these names common gold, silver, or quicksilver, which are "dead" and cannot be usefully employed in our work. 

I say this so that no one is deceived, like that "great Operator" who hung his vessel containing his matter at the top of an exposed pillar to receive the moisture, which he called the **spit of the Moon** <note>A poetic alchemical term for dew or the condensed "celestial" moisture believed to have transformative properties.</note>, and by day, the **heat of the Sun**. Now, our Author does not say, "*it is the spit of the Moon*," but rather "*it is called Moon’s spit*," and so on for the others. 

Now, because many—after having bundled together various pieces without profit other than the lightening of their purses—have finally resolved to wait patiently for revelation from above, believing that just as the Angel **Uriel** came to enlighten **Ezra** <note>The author refers to the apocryphal book of 2 Esdras (or 4 Ezra), where the angel Uriel provides divine wisdom.</note> regarding the visions he had seen (to whom he also discovered that powder of which he speaks in chapter 8 of the 4th book, saying that a little of it makes much gold): in the same way, this good Angel will unseal their eyes, open their ears, and lead them by the hand to the desired path. ...properly through the fire of friendship <note>original: *feu d’amitié*; likely referring to a gentle, steady heat (like a water bath) that allows substances to blend without destruction</note>; and this matter has for its mother the Sun and the Moon <term>Sun and Moon: Alchemical gold and silver, or the active/sulfuric and passive/mercurial principles</term>, which have engendered it by means of the wind that carries it along with it, and covers itself with it as with a crepe or mantle <note>This is a direct allusion to the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus: "The wind carried it in its belly."</note>, borrowing the name of "vinegar," and other names because of its actions. Thus, this matter remained for a very long time without form, contrary to the doctrine of the common Philosophers who do not admit any matter without form. Then, when this composition of matter and form is united, it is named *white earth* or *red earth* according to its resulting color. 

However, let no one be so ill-advised as to join and cook common quicksilver and common sulfur together, for they will have nothing from this mixture but what is commonly called *cinnabar* <note>Cinnabar is a common mineral, mercury sulfide; the author warns that laboratory chemistry alone does not equal the "Great Work."</note>. I know this for having seen it done by a friend of mine, and against my opinion, which was overruled by the oaths and assurances of one who called himself very learned and expert in this science; who, having had two hundred ducatons <note>Large silver coins used in Europe from the 16th to 18th centuries</note> filed down, mixed them with an equal weight of cinnabar, and at the

<meta>This page follows a warning from the previous page against the folly of mixing common silver with common cinnabar. It provides a practical anecdote (a "cautionary tale") of an experiment that failed to produce any alchemical transformation, proving the author's earlier point.</meta>

### CHAPTER I.

After eight days of cooking, once everything had cooled, it was poured into a vessel: truly, the said filed silver was found to weigh exactly its original weight, and the cinnabar <term>cinnabar: a bright red mineral consisting of mercury sulfide, often used in attempts to create "philosophical" mercury</term> increased in weight by about twenty ounces, so much so that this "great expert," looking at me, said, "Well now, what do you say?" Then, without answering him, I asked the Goldsmith if he wished to buy this silver that he himself had filed. To which he replied, "It is true that I filed it, but when I filed it, it was white, and now it is very black; nonetheless, I will buy it once it has been melted." Having done this on the spot and cast it into an ingot, he found his ingot diminished by about twenty-two ounces from its original weight, and very low on the touchstone <term>touchstone: a small tablet of dark stone used to test the purity of gold and silver by the color of the streak they leave when rubbed upon it</term>. And having melted the cinnabar that was said to have increased by twenty ounces in weight, it was found that the whole weighed only about nine ounces; and if the whole had been put to the cupel <term>cupel: a shallow, porous bowl used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals by heating</term>, the loss would have been much greater. Thus was this "great Doctor" confounded by the proof, so that the cinnabar, the vessel, and the charcoal were all lost, and the silver diminished in both value and weight—of which Trevisan <note>Bernard of Treviso (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist whose writings warned against common chemical errors</note> had already given warning in his *Opuscule* <note>original: *Opuscule*; refers to a "Little Work" or short treatise</note> when speaking of the "nails of cinnabar" <note>likely a reference to a specific fraudulent alchemical process or "fixation" attempted by chemists in Paris</note> that were made in Paris.

<meta>Following the account of the failed experiment with silver and cinnabar on the previous page, the author now moves from practical anecdote to theoretical explanation. He uses the writings of Bernard of Treviso to explain how the four elements—specifically the relationship between "dryness" and "fluidity"—govern the behavior of chemical substances like mercury.</meta>

->CHEMICAL HARMONY<-

...is the fire, symbolically in dryness. Now, they have said that water and air are spirits, because they are elements that moisten and dissolve the earth; calling air and fire <margin>ripen</margin> Soul, because they mature <note>The original French text reads "meurent" (die), but the marginal note "murissent" (ripen/mature) corrects this to describe the active, perfecting role of the "Soul" elements.</note>, digest, and complete the entire compound. Note, however, that he who speaks of "Soul" speaks metaphorically; the same is understood of "Spirit." It is not as a vegetative soul, nor is the "Body" a forming form <term>forming form: (forme formante) a philosophical term referring to the internal principle that gives a living thing its specific shape and functions</term>, such as that found in humans and other sentient beings. Therefore, the said Philosophers <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists, often called "Philosophers of Nature."</note> should not be understood literally, but according to the possibilities of nature. Trevisan to Thomas of Bologna, pp. 166–167.

### Scholium.

If one places a measure of water upon an equal measure of ashes, the ashes will arrest the fluidity of the water through their dryness <term>dryness: (siccité) one of the four primary qualities in ancient physics; here it acts as a stabilizing force</term>. This is what Trevisan tells us here, calling the earth the *body and bones of it*, which prevents the quicksilver <term>quicksilver: (vif argent) another name for mercury</term>, mercury, and other chemical elements from flowing—not only upon a flat surface, but even during ordinary tests; that is to say, from escaping either during simple melting or in the cupel <term>cupel: a porous vessel used in refining to separate precious metals from impurities</term>. This is because this "earth" is hot and dry, a symbol of fire, and therefore dries out the moisture of the matter. The rest is clear enough and has been sufficiently explained previously.

<summary>The author explains the alchemical symbolism of the four elements, defining "Soul" and "Spirit" as metaphors for active qualities rather than biological life. He cites Bernard of Treviso to illustrate how the "dryness" of earth stabilizes the "fluidity" of mercury during chemical heating.</summary>
<keywords>Bernard of Treviso, four elements, alchemical soul, dryness, quicksilver, mercury, Thomas of Bologna, cupellation, symbolic chemistry</keywords>

<meta>Building on the previous page's discussion of dryness and fluidity, this page moves into specific alchemical allegories. It shifts from Trevisan’s general theories to a specific recipe or "text" taken from an unidentified "old manuscript," followed by a scholarly commentary (Scholium) that decodes these symbols into the standard alchemical triad of Mercury, the Sun (Gold), and the Moon (Silver).</meta>

CHAPTER I.
C iiij

### TEXT.

<margin>An old manuscript.</margin>
Take the very pure air, the colored fire, and the radiant water, and mix them. Our dung <note>original: "fumier." In alchemy, "dung" often refers to the base matter undergoing putrefaction, or sometimes to the gentle, moist heat of a manure pile used for digestion.</note> is quicksilver <term>quicksilver: (argent vif) the common name for mercury</term>, and it is so called because its natural moisture (by reason of its oiliness) is preserved for a long time from putrefaction by its own heat. Now, the Philosophers have attributed several names to the matter because of the various degrees of cooking; for when Mercury is cold and moist, it is called Soul; when the fire has consumed its moisture and this same Mercury is dry, it is called Spirit; and furthermore, when it is fixed by a greater decoction <term>decoction: the process of heating a substance in a liquid to extract its essence or change its state</term>, it is called Body. Thus, one and the same thing is called by three different names. From an old manuscript.

---

->Scholium.<-

<margin>
<image-desc>A vertical wavy line bracketed next to the first paragraph, and a handwritten flourish or signature "Lu" in the lower right margin.</image-desc>
</margin>
This Author is clear enough, showing that the composition commonly called the Philosophers' Stone is made of very pure air, which is Mercury; of colored fire, which is the Sun <note>In alchemical symbolism, the Sun (Sol) represents Gold.</note>; and of radiant water, which is the Moon <note>In alchemical symbolism, the Moon (Luna) represents Silver.</note>. By these three, as I have said, one must not understand common quicksilver, gold, and silver, but those known and understood by the Philochymists <term>Philochymists: (Philochymiques) literally "lovers of chemistry," a term for those who study the spiritual and physical transformation of matter</term>, commonly called Philosophers.

<note>This page emphasizes the distinction between "vulgar" materials found in a marketplace and "philosophical" materials prepared by the alchemist. The transformation of one substance through three stages—Soul, Spirit, and Body—reflects the alchemical goal of "fixing" the volatile mercury into a stable, solid form.</note>

<summary>This page presents a cryptic recipe from an old manuscript involving "air, fire, and water," which the commentary identifies as the philosophical versions of Mercury, Gold, and Silver. It explains how a single substance changes its name from Soul to Spirit to Body based on its degree of heat and dryness.</summary>

CHEMICAL HARMONY

### Text.

<margin>Correction of Fools <note>original Latin: *Correctio fatuorum*, a title of an alchemical treatise often attributed to Richardus Anglicus or pseudo-Geber.</note></margin>
The dissolution of bodies is twofold: namely into mercury and into the mercurial. The first is for particulars <note>In alchemy, "particulars" refers to processes aimed at limited transmutations or specific metallic changes, rather than the "Universal" work of the Great Elixir.</note>, the second for universals. The first is nothing other than a resolution; the second is by the putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the chemical breakdown of a substance, often associated with the color black and the "death" of the metal before its rebirth</term> of the body and the spirit into moisture. Now, putrefaction is the solution and separation of all natures bound one to another. *Correction of Fools, p. 15.*

### Scholium.

Here is a Master who in appearance speaks in a manner unlike the others, though he does not; saying that the dissolution of bodies is twofold. We have already said that dissolution is the separation of the parts or of the virtue of bodies—as with a tree that one separates into bark, trunk, leaves, and other parts, and from these same parts one extracts water, oil, salt, and earth by the alembic <term>alembic: a distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube</term> or by fire. The first dissolution necessary for this work is into mercury—that is to say, into blackness, or if one prefers, into liquid matter to which the sun <note>Gold</note> and the moon <note>Silver</note> are reduced by the amalgam of the first mercury. This is altogether nothing but a common confusion or mixture, through which one arrives at this second Mercury or first Saturn <note>In this context, Saturn refers to the "Lead of the Philosophers" or the black state of the matter, not common lead.</note> by the aid of a gentle fire; and this solution is only for particulars, name- <note>The text cuts off mid-word: "asça-" for *asçavoir* (namely).</note>

# CHAPTER I.

...to have for the Sun <note>Gold</note> and for the Moon <note>Silver</note> which must give this blackness; but the second dissolution, which is into *Mercurial Water*, non-wetting nor flowing (as many believe, and as we have said before) is white or red matter, which is for the universals <note>Processes intended to create the "Universal Medicine" or the Elixir, rather than simple metallic transmutations.</note>, which it depurates and renders in a brilliant and permanent whiteness or redness. The first is thus nothing other than a simple resolution of the bodies of the Sun and the Moon by means of Mercury (and aided by a proper fire) and their seed, sulfur, or tincture; and the second is a putrefaction <term>putrefaction: the stage of the alchemical process where the matter decomposes and turns black, signaling the "death" of the old form before its rebirth</term>, or perfect mixture of the *blackness drawn from the said Sun and Moon with the philosophical mercury*, which gives to this blackness—which is an impalpable powder, dry and unctuous—ingress <term>ingress: the power of the alchemical medicine to penetrate and "enter" into the heart of base metals to transform them</term> and entry into the metals that one wishes to purify. 

Now, *putrefaction* is nothing other than the *solution or separation* of all things bound one to another, which will be easy to understand if we pay heed to the authorities alleged above; for all the authors say that by means of fire, the body which was moist and black becomes white and dry, and afterwards red and fixed. The moisture, therefore, which was bound with the blackness, dries out, and the blackness which hid the whiteness withdraws and disappears; and the white which covered the red, and which was only dried, disappears at the appearance of the red, so that within one and the same very well-closed vessel, the moisture is dried and the dryness is fixed, the black is whitened, and the white is reddened without any separation of parts or of elements (but only the addition of its principle, or more proximate [principle]...)

# CHAPTER I.

sky, *red sulfur*, *gold*, *carbuncle* <note>original: *efcarboucle*; a legendary deep red gemstone, often used as a metaphor for the final, perfected state of the Philosopher's Stone</note>, and by the name of all precious and beautiful red things, whether animals, stones, or plants. ### Scholium
<note>A scholium is an explanatory note or commentary intended to clarify a specific point in a complex text.</note>

<margin>
Correct reading: Good
</margin>

Our Author teaches us that what the Philosophers call *latten* <term>latten: original French *laton*; in alchemy, this refers to a base metal or an impure "blackened" alloy that must be purified until it shines like gold</term> is nothing common, nor a composite as many imagine. But just as red copper, when melted with calamine <note>a zinc-bearing mineral used in the historical production of brass</note> or calamite, loses its redness and becomes yellow, so too the latten of the Philosophers—which is *a body composed of the Sun <note>Gold</note> and Moon <note>Silver</note>*, and which he calls *copper with Mercury*—loses its luster and indeed its name. It is then neither gold nor silver, neither Sun nor Moon, but everything. For the first, namely the *latten*, is nothing other than the union of the two bodies; but the second, which is the *copper* <note>original: *l'airain*</note>, is the body ready to be dissolved. 

Let it not be thought a matter of doubt when he says "or," for it is as if he said: *latten* is what some have called by such a name, and not by another. But here is a question of little difficulty: namely, whether *sulfur* and *latten* are different things. To this one may answer that *latten* is the composition, and acts as the "ore" from which the sulfur emerges. Here, *sulfur* is taken not to mean the blackness <note>the *nigredo* or initial stage of decay</note>, but the whiteness or redness. Because this sulfur is still volatile <note>meaning it would evaporate or change if heat were applied</note>, one must join to it *the leaven*—that is to say, the Sun or the Moon—as will be seen hereafter in its proper place.

<note>original vocabulary: red sulfur, gold, carbuncle, latten, red copper, calamine, calamite, Sun, Moon, copper, Mercury, leaven</note>
