<meta>This page transitions from the specific alchemical definitions of "Magnesia" and "latten" discussed on page 44 to a broader philosophical instruction on how to read alchemical texts. It opens Chapter 1 of a new section or treatise, focusing on the role of reason and intellect over literal interpretation.</meta>

# CHAPTER I.

<meta>Catchword: [sc]iences; handwritten number 167 in margin; ink bleed from reverse page</meta>

### ->*TEXT*<-

<margin>St. Thomas.</margin>
Pay little heed to the words of modern or ancient Philosophers when they speak of this science, for the entirety of the art consists in the capacity of the intellect and in demonstrative experience. Because the Philosophers wished to hide the truths of the science, they have described all things by speaking in figures <note>original: "figures"; referring to metaphors, allegories, or symbolic diagrams</note>. Thomas to Brother Reynaud, Chapter 1. <note>This refers to a work traditionally attributed to Thomas Aquinas addressed to his companion, Reginald of Piperno.</note>

### ->*Scholia*<-

<margin>Note all of this carefully</margin>
This is yet another lesson for those who cling to the literal words and to the "bark" <note>the outer surface or superficial meaning of a text</note> without wishing to penetrate any deeper. It shows briefly that both the young and the old have set themselves to hiding this science. Though it is a natural science (as we have already stated), it must be carefully researched, pursued, and grasped by the spirit and by reason. It is reason that must judge, for example, whether a man can be begotten from the seed of a dog, or if a rabbit could grow from the germ of a mallow plant <note>a common flowering herb</note>, or if a volatile <term>volatile: a substance that easily evaporates or escapes when heated</term> thing can engender something manageable and fixed <term>fixed: a substance that remains stable and solid even when exposed to intense fire</term>. Reason must judge how a completely new thing can be made from another even newer thing, without the destruction of that first new thing, but rather its preservation. Indeed, it is necessary that the spirit be at play, and that reason—which is the mistress of all experiences—works just as powerfully.

<vocab>Philosophers, intellect, demonstrative experience, Thomas to Brother Reynaud, natural science, reason</vocab>

<summary>This page warns the reader against literal interpretations of alchemical texts, emphasizing that the "art" relies on intellectual reasoning and practical experience rather than the cryptic "figures" used by ancient philosophers. It argues that while alchemy is a natural science, it requires a sharp mind to distinguish between natural possibilities and absurdities.</summary>
<keywords>Thomas Aquinas, Brother Reginald, allegory, reason vs. experience, natural science, volatile and fixed, intellect, alchemical symbols</keywords>

<margin>St. Thomas.</margin>
Do not concern yourself much with the words of modern or ancient Philosophers speaking of this science, insofar as the whole art consists in the capacity of the intellect and in demonstrative experience; for the Philosophers, wanting to hide the truths of the science, have marked everything by speaking in figures <note>original: "figures"; symbolic or metaphorical language used to conceal secrets from the uninitiated</note>. <margin>Note all this carefully</margin>
This is yet another lesson for those who cling to words and to the bark <note>The "bark" (French: "escorce") refers to the superficial or literal meaning of a text, as opposed to the internal "pith" or hidden truth.</note> without wanting to penetrate further, showing briefly that both the young and the old have taken pleasure in hiding this science. Although it is a natural science (as we have already said), it must be carefully researched, pursued, and apprehended by the spirit and by reason. It is reason that must judge: for instance, whether a man can be fathered from the seed of a dog; whether a fir tree can rise high from the germ of a mallow <note>a common flowering plant</note>; or whether a volatile thing can beget a manageable and fixed thing. It must judge how a thing entirely new can be made from another even newer, without the destruction of that first novelty, but rather its preservation. In this, the spirit must necessarily play its part, and reason—which is the mistress of all experiences—must work just as powerfully.

<image-desc>A vertical line with a cross-bar, resembling a stylized dagger or cross, often used as a sign for a crucible or a marker for a key point in the text.</image-desc>

*The primary matter of bodies is not **common mercury** <note>original: "mercure vulgaire"; the standard quicksilver available to merchants, which alchemists viewed as "dead" compared to their "living" Philosophic Mercury</note>, but it is an unctuous and moist vapor: for the **mineral stone** is made from the moist, and the metallic body from the unctuous; and it is necessary that the bodies be converted into such an unctuous vapor. In this conversion the bodies die, and the grain of the body dies entirely; and this is done by the way of our **white and red water** <note>These refer to the solvents used to reach the "Albedo" (white stage) and "Rubedo" (red stage) of the Great Work</note>. This vapor is called the "stone," known through our books as the principle of the matter of our operation and **unctuous sulfur**, from which the **quintessence** <term>The "fifth essence" or purest spiritual core of a substance</term> is first extracted, and the mercury dyeing every body into **Sun** or **Moon** <note>Common alchemical code for Gold and Silver</note>, according to how it is prepared at the very end. The same [author], page 180.*

*Our stone is composed of body, spirit, and soul; for the imperfect body is called "body," the leaven is called "soul," and the water is called "spirit." The imperfect body is by its nature heavy, infirm, and dead; the water is a spirit that purges, refines, and whitens the body; the leaven is the soul which gives life to the imperfect body—a life it did not have before—and gives it a better form. The body is **Venus** <note>Symbolizing copper or the receptive, female principle in the chemical marriage</note> and female.*

<vocab>potable gold, common mercury, mineral stone, white water, red water, quintessence, Sun (Gold), Moon (Silver), body, spirit, soul, leaven, Venus (Copper)</vocab>

<summary>This page defines the "stone" as a refined vapor rather than common mercury and explains the alchemical trinity of Body, Spirit, and Soul as the components required to give "life" to dead metal.</summary>
<keywords>potable gold, primary matter, unctuous vapor, white and red water, Sun and Moon, alchemical trinity, Venus, quintessence</keywords>

...the spirit is mercury & male, the soul is Sun & Moon. The same [author], page 187.

The **Dragon** <note>In alchemical iconography, the Dragon represents the volatile "mercury of the philosophers"—the raw, chaotic material that must be "tamed" or "killed" to be transformed into the Stone.</note> is the **quicksilver** <term>Argent vif: literally "live silver," the common name for the liquid metal mercury</term> drawn from bodies, having within itself body, soul, & spirit, of which the Philosopher, speaking, says that the Dragon does not die without his brother & his sister—that is to say, without the **Sun** and the **Moon** <note>original: "Soleil & la Lune"; these are the alchemical archetypes for Gold (masculine/hot) and Silver (feminine/cold).</note>. The same [author], page 188.

The **Sun** is "animal" <note>The author classifies the Sun/Gold as "animal" not because it is a creature, but because it possesses a "living" quality that allows it to react, grow, and be "fed" by other substances during the work.</note>, because it receives restriction, whitening, & reddening, and is like a Great Animal, and **sal ammoniac** <term>A pungent salt used as a flux to clean metals and aid in chemical reactions</term> is made from it; the **Moon** is named "flat," and **alkali salt** <note>original: "sel khaiy"; likely a phonetic or archaic spelling of "alkali," often derived from the ashes of the Kali plant.</note> is made from her; **mercury** is called "mineral earth," and common salt is made from [it]...

### CHAPTER I.

The Philosophers have given several names to the stone, to the end that while it is manifest to the wise, the foolish do not know it; but however it may be named, it is always one, and of the same matter. The same [author], page 256.

->Scholium<-
<note>A scholium is a marginal note or explanatory commentary on a text.</note>

Our present Author tells us that even though several names are given to the matter, it is nonetheless not multiple, but unique and originating from one and the same thing; this is what the aforementioned sages have already noted. Now, if our matter did not come from another [source] that is pure and fixed, it could not be so itself: for one has never seen a bad tree produce good fruit, nor a fir tree produce oranges, nor a nettle a melon, since one cannot introduce into nature—that is to say, into a thing—anything that does not suit it; but since our matter emerges from another that is pure and fixed, the order of nature is that it should be so as well. It is true that the matters from which ours proceeds differ in species, such as the male and the female, but they agree in genus. Now, to show that the names do not make the thing different, he adds that *the juice of moonwort* <note>original: "suc de lunaire"; a plant (Botrychium lunaria) believed by alchemists to have lunar properties and used in "vegetable" alchemy</note>, *the water of life* <term>Eau de vie: literally "water of life"; here referring to a potent alchemical solvent or spirit rather than modern brandy</term>, *the quintessence of burning wine*, and *vegetable mercury* <note>The vital essence or "spirit" extracted from plant matter</note> are one and the same thing. I cannot marvel enough at the infinity of seekers of this precious matter, who, not paying attention to these...

<summary>This page discusses the unity of the "First Matter," explaining that although the Sages use many names to hide the secret from the "foolish," the substance remains one. It argues that the matter must come from a pure source, following the laws of nature where like produces like.</summary>

# CHEMICAL HARMONY

...[fools] <note>The OCR "mots" is likely a partial transcription of "sots" (fools).</note> struggle to seek the plant named moonwort <note>original: "herbe lunaire"; the plant *Botrychium lunaria*, which practitioners of "vegetable alchemy" believed could fix mercury into silver due to its crescent-shaped leaves.</note>, in order to fix mercury with it, resulting in the loss of money and time; as well as to rectify the <term>water of life<note>original: "au de vie" (eau de vie); here referring to a highly distilled alchemical spirit or solvent rather than modern brandy.</note></term> to such a degree that one can find almost no vessel suitable to hold it. As for the quintessence, since its matter is not described here, everyone imagines one for themselves: and noting how many charlatans there are who, for excessive sums of money, promise its recipe, and recounting the fable of Demosthenes <note>Likely referring to the "Shadow of the Ass," a fable Demosthenes told to an inattentive audience to show they preferred trifles to matters of life and death.</note>, they play upon the ignorance that reigns in the midst of those who believe themselves learned, by whom they are better heard than those who speak only with sound reason. 

O you stupid ones, how long will you sleep? The lunar juice is drawn not from the plant called moonwort—be it the large or the small variety—but from our wine, which is known only to the true children of science; it is with this juice, or mercury, that our solution or blackness <note>The *nigredo* stage, where the material decomposes and turns black before it can be purified.</note> is made, just as our ***potable gold*** is likewise made. Away then with all these madmen, who point to another potable gold, another solution, or another matter than ours, without which we will never have anything good in this search: but do not imagine that it is common <term>quicksilver<note>original: "argent vif vulgaire"; standard metallic mercury, which alchemists distinguished from their "Philosophical Mercury."</note></term>, as the common sort of seekers believe, inasmuch as it is a moist and oily vapor, which occurs through our artifice upon our composition; and this black vapor, adhering to the fingers when handled, in the form of a black and very subtle powder into which the bodies are converted, and they die—not in the manner of a grain of wheat or other plant, but of an animal at the time of coitus <note>original: "au temps du cit"; likely a typo for *coït*, the alchemical "marriage" or union of male and female principles.</note>—and this vapor having been converted by the required order into

### CHAPTER I.

[white or red] <note>This completes the sentence from the previous page: "another [white or red] color."</note> color is named water; being cast upon the impure bodies, it kills them, that is to say, it totally deprives and strips them of their first being, giving them another entirely new one, and thus a new form and a new life of gold and silver. This method is known in the books; away then once again, enemies of doctrine, who forbid the reading of books in order to dearly sell your charlatanry, which is exposed by them. Let us say then, our earth is composed of three parts: namely, the imperfect body, which is the blackness drawn—as we have said—from the perfect bodies (specifically the gold and silver of the Philosophers, which are the <term>leaven<note>original: "leuain"; a substance added to the "paste" of the alchemical work to trigger transformation, similar to how yeast ferments dough.</note></term>), and of Mercury, which is the spirit. So that it may be thus, he adds: 

<margin>
<image-desc>A hand-drawn marginal doodle consisting of two wavy vertical lines above a small circle, with three dots descending below it.</image-desc>
</margin>

*the imperfect body, namely the blackness, is a heavy, infirm body*, for of itself it can do nothing; *black is the hieroglyphic of death*, but mercury is a spirit that <term>subtilizes<note>To "subtilize" is to refine a solid substance into a spiritual or gaseous state.</note></term> and whitens doubly; for gold placed within it is whitened there, as are all the other metals, rendering themselves into small and white parts. Our blackness is re-whitened into the whiteness of snow, and the silver or gold serving as leaven to this paste rises to a better state than it was; and from being <term>volatile<note>original: "volatille"; a substance that easily evaporates or escapes when heated.</note></term> as it was, it becomes <term>fixed<note>original: "fixe"; a substance that remains stable and solid even under the highest heat of a furnace.</note></term>, which gives it, as it were, another life. The fifth corollary is intelligible enough, and by which what is written above is confirmed; for although the Philosophers seem to contradict one another, nevertheless, whoever pays close attention finds no opposition there, as has been sufficiently said; we say the same of the others.

<margin>Dastinus.</margin>
Have no worry about the diversity of names, nor the diversity of <term>regimes<note>In alchemy, a "regimen" refers to the specific method of applying heat and managing the stages of the chemical process.</note></term>, for as much as if we wish to make the <term>Sun<note>original: "Soleil"; the alchemical symbol for Gold.</note></term>, we put the Sun into it; if the <term>Moon<note>original: "Lune"; the alchemical symbol for Silver.</note></term>, the Moon; as <term>leaven<note>original: "leuain"; a substance added to the alchemical "paste" to trigger a transformation, much like yeast in dough.</note></term>, the fire is the black earth. ### Scholia.

<margin>There is some difficulty here which will be removed hereafter.</margin>
One must observe that our author, in saying *Sun* & *Moon*, adds *as leaven*, to the end that no one should presume he means this for the beginning of the work; <note>The author clarifies that gold and silver are added as catalysts (leaven) later in the process, not as the raw starting materials.</note> now, from the black powder of which he speaks, two parts are made: one floating on top, like a subtle veil [crepe], which can only be drawn out with its body that is useless to the work, and this is what he calls air or oil, or water; but the other part which goes to the bottom by a subtle means is that which is most prized, and which the <term>Philosophers<note>The "Philosophers" refers to the established lineage of alchemists and natural philosophers from antiquity to the Renaissance.</note></term> call their black earth, which, when nourished white or red, and completed, converts metals to its nature.

The stone is named air, which is mixed with its earth, and <term>salamander<note>In the alchemical tradition, the salamander is a mythical creature that lives in fire; here, it symbolizes a substance that has become "fixed" and can withstand intense heat without evaporating.</note></term>, because it is nourished by fire alone. *Flower of Flowers* p. 37.

### Scholia.

<image-desc>A large, hand-drawn decorative flourish or bracket consisting of two long, wavy vertical lines with a small heart-like loop in the center, positioned in the right margin.</image-desc>

### Text.

When our black matter becomes white, it is called <term>foliated earth<note>original: "terre folliée." A stage in the process where the material takes on a flaky, layered, or crystalline appearance like the leaves of a book.</note></term>, ash of ashes, ferment of ferment, and white sulfur capable of enduring the fire; however, one will have neither Sun nor Moon without <term>leaven<note>original: "leuain." A symbolic name for the essential solvent or acid that dissolves the raw materials at the start of the work.</note></term>, and is the sign of true <term>dissolution<note>The chemical stage where the solid "body" is broken down into its "spirit."</note></term>. *From the Book of the Three Words* p. 48.

->54<-

### Text.

<margin>Dastin <note>original: "Dastinus." John Dastin (c. 1293–1386) was an English alchemist famous for his correspondence with Pope John XXII defending the possibility of transmutation.</note></margin>
Have no concern for the diversity of names, nor for the diversity of methods, for as much as if we wish to make the **Sun** <note>Gold</note>, we put into it the **Sun**; if the **Moon** <note>Silver</note>, the **Moon**; as for the **leaven**, the fire is the **black earth**. Dastin p. 30.

That which rises to the top is called **air** and **oil**, and that which is at the bottom is named **pure fire**, because our **earth** is named **fire**, and our **water** is named **oil** which is not consumed by the dryness of the fire. The same author, p. 31.

### Scholia.

<margin>There is some difficulty here which will be removed hereafter.</margin>
It must be observed that our author, in saying *Sun* and *Moon*, adds *for leaven*, to the end that no one should presume that he means this to be the very beginning of the work <note>Alchemists often distinguished between the "beginning" (raw materials) and the "leavening" stage, where gold or silver was added to the already-prepared "Stone" to give it a specific direction.</note>. Now, from the **black powder** of which he speaks, two parts are made: one floating on top, like a **subtle veil** <note>original: "crespe." A thin, gauze-like film or scum that appears on the surface of the liquid during the chemical reaction.</note>, which can only be extracted along with its body (which is useless to the work), and this is what he calls **air** or **oil**, or **water**. But the other part, which goes to the bottom by a subtle means, is that which is most prized, and which the **Philosophers** <note>A common term for alchemists.</note> call their **black earth**; this, when nourished until it becomes white or red and is perfected, converts metals into its own nature.

->*Scholia.*<-

What we have clarified above is sufficient for the understanding of these three passages, which speak no other language than all the others.

->*Text.*<-

<margin>
*The Lord be with you.* <note>original: "Dominus vobiscum." A common liturgical greeting used here as the title of an alchemical treatise, possibly the "Liber Dominus Vobiscum" often attributed to Raymund Lully or a similar medieval authority.</note>
<insert>232</insert>
</margin>
**Magnesia** <note>In alchemy, "Magnesia" does not refer to the modern element magnesium, but to the "First Matter" or the crude, dark substance from which the Philosopher's Stone is extracted.</note> is the general matter containing the **four elements**; **mercury**, cooked in such a way that it has not lost its **fiery virtue**, is a perfect body without any exhalation <note>Meaning it has been stabilized so it no longer evaporates or "fumes" away when heated.</note>. Because of its various stages, it is called by various names: for while it is cold and humid, it is called **soul**; when it is dry, it is called **spirit**; being more cooked and fixed, it is called **body**, and it is not fixed except by great **decoction** <note>A process of prolonged heating in a liquid or moist state.</note>. It is a very clear thing that mercury will be perfected sooner by adding the **bodies** <note>Usually referring to the "perfect" metals, gold and silver.</note> to it than by remaining alone, which the Philosophers confirm, saying and calling the **Sun** <note>Gold</note> the brother of mercury and the **Moon** <note>Silver</note> its sister. And whoever joins me with my brother or my sister will nourish as many thousands of men for as long as he wishes, which he will achieve by making the male impregnate the female. *Book of the "The Lord be with you," p. 50.*

<term>Magnesia: the primary material of the work</term>, <term>four elements: earth, air, fire, water</term>, <term>mercury: the fluid principle of transformation</term>, <term>fiery virtue: the inherent heat or energy within the matter</term>, <term>soul: the volatile/moist stage</term>, <term>spirit: the dry/penetrating stage</term>, <term>body: the fixed/solid stage</term>, <term>decoction: the act of refining through heat</term>, <term>Philosophers: the alchemical masters of old</term>, <term>Sun: gold</term>, <term>Moon: silver</term>, <term>The Lord be with you: the title of the cited treatise</term>

It emphasizes that Mercury is perfected through union with the "Sun" (gold) and "Moon" (silver), using the metaphor of a brother and sister or a male impregnating a female to achieve the multiplication of the Stone.</summary>

# CHAPTER I.

From the beginning, our **stone** <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the Great Work, believed to transform base metals into gold.</note> is called **water**; the **body** being dissolved and tending toward consolidation is called **earth**; being perfected and fixed, it is called **fire**. *The same, p. 54.*

->Scholia.<-

We have already spoken of **Magnesia** <term>Magnesia: In alchemy, this is not the modern element magnesium, but the "First Matter"—the raw, chaotic substance from which the Stone is extracted.</term>, which contains the **four elements**, namely **water**, **earth**, **air**, and **fire**, or the **black**, the **white**, the **yellow**, and the **red** <note>These colors correspond to the four major stages of the Great Work: Nigredo (blackness), Albedo (whiteness), Citrinitas (yellowness), and Rubedo (redness).</note>. Let us note, however, that the **cooked mercury** spoken of here—which has not lost its **fiery force and virtue**—is called by an infinity of names when it is perfect. This is nothing other than the matter which has been extracted, blackened, then whitened, then reddened, and finally made fit to perfect the **imperfect metals** <note>Base metals like lead or tin.</note> by the order that will be noted hereafter in its proper place.

->Text.<-

<margin>uncertain</margin>
**Quicksilver** <note>Mercury.</note> is named **wind**, that is to say, **aerial quicksilver**, **strong vinegar**, **strong water** <note>original: "eau forte." In alchemy, this often refers to a powerful solvent like nitric acid used to dissolve metals.</note>, **dyeing venom**, **virgin’s milk**, **fountain of life**, and **burning fire**. *An uncertain Author, p. 66.*

<margin>Saturn</margin>
**Putrefaction** <note>The process of breaking down and decaying the matter, usually the first step of the work.</note> takes place at the bottom of the vessel, and **generation** <note>The birth or emergence of the new, purified substance.</note> at the head of the **alembic** <term>Alembic: A classic distillation apparatus consisting of two vessels connected by a tube, used to evaporate and condense substances.</term>. **Quicksilver** is called, in the generation of metals: **father**, **true life**, **Lion**, **Phoenix**, **Pelican**, **Tantalus**, **Daedalus**, **Serpent**, and **Fountain**.

In this vessel, the truly foul-smelling little vapors, unable to escape, are trapped; through a gentle fire, these vapors engender our matter. To this matter, the name **quicksilver** and many others are attributed, such as **Father**, since engendering a new form is the same as giving new life. Like a strong **Lion**, it drives out the impurities of the so-called imperfect metals <note>Base metals like lead or tin.</note>. It is the only thing that can be self-engendered in this way—what is called making the **Phoenix** <note>The mythical bird that rises from its own ashes, symbolizing the cycle of death and rebirth in the alchemical work.</note>—and that can perform such purification and nourish those of its own kind or species.

### Scholium.

If it is true (as indeed it is) that all the colors, and consequently the completion of our entire work, occur through a single process, why then are there so many vessels, so many furnaces, and so many various operations proposed by the charlatans? These men are totally ignorant of the intention and understanding of the wise <term>Philochymists</term> <note>From the Greek, meaning "lovers of chemistry/alchemy."</note>, who desire that from their composition there be produced a *black mud*, stinking, impalpable <note>Too fine to be felt as distinct particles.</note>, unctuous, and subtle. This mud, being washed with its own "water" (which it drinks and reduces, like itself, into an impalpable powder), turns white and then red; and having been whitened or reddened, it purifies the so-called "impure" metals. Now this mud, which is drawn from the two astralized and radiant bodies, is called *shadow* or *dryness* <note>original: "siccité"</note>, hidden within the bodies of the *Sun* <note>Symbolizing Gold.</note> and the *Moon* <note>Symbolizing Silver.</note> which have been made humid by the <term>amalgam</term> <note>A blend of metals, usually involving mercury.</note> to which the artist has reduced them. By this amalgam, the entire mass is rendered with a radiant whiteness and becomes as a single matter. This is the reason why he <note>Referring to Hermes, mentioned on the previous page.</note> does not say that one must draw the shadow from the "rays" [plural], but from "its ray" [singular]; showing that this *shadow* or *blackness* <note>The text ends mid-word with the catchword "noir-" (black-), indicating the continuation on the next page: "noirceur."</note> must come from the union of the two, and not from them separately.

<summary>This page explains that the alchemical "Stone" is called a "pregnant woman" because the white stage already contains the red stage within it. It critiques the complexity of fraudulent alchemists, arguing that the true work relies on a single process that produces a transformative "black mud" from the union of gold and silver.</summary>

blackness <note>original: "noirceur"</note> be drawn out: This *Shadow, blackness, or dryness* <term>dryness</term> <note>original: "siccité"; the active, masculine principle in alchemy, often associated with fire and sulfur.</note> is named *male* or *agent*, because it arrests <note>In alchemy, to "arrest" or "fix" means to transform a volatile, liquid substance (like mercury) into a solid, stable form that can withstand fire.</note> the common mercury, when they are mixed according to a procedure known by few, and sought by many researchers; and if this dryness, blackness, or masculinity were not hidden within the moisture, one could not draw it out from there. Now when he says that *this moisture is dried out*, one must not understand it as being drawn away by the Sun or air—like the moisture of a linen cloth which remains lighter after it has dried—but in this case, the water applied to this *mud or blackness or sulfur* remains there (if the vessel is well sealed) and is there reduced into the same form, which is this *sulfur*, namely into powder, so much so that the quantity of this mud increases and its quality is reinforced; on one hand it is well governed, but if it is poorly managed, the volatility will carry everything away above the fire. For if that person who is deemed so learned in all sciences in Paris had well understood this passage, he would not have put and kept his matter—which was only common gold—for the space of seven years in the fire and furnace of the glassworks <note>Glassmaking furnaces were favored by some alchemists because they provided intense, continuous heat over long periods.</note>, where I saw him with his vessel, and in the said furnace. The matter is named **elementary stone**, inasmuch as the four elements are drawn from it; **mineral stone**, because it is made of minerals alone; **vegetable stone**, because it is nourished and increases, which are powers of the vegetative soul; **animal stone**, because it is restored by scent and corrupted by stench; and **rational stone**, because it subsists, in harmony with nature, when it reaches the final goal. ### CHAPTER I.

solids, fixed, lengthening and extending under the hammer, and becoming more pleasing through the violence of fire; and from these, I say, when assembled, a matter is produced which is like them neither in solidity, nor in consistency, nor in color, nor in odor—unknown to everyone, I say even to its practitioner, if it were taken and put into one vessel, then removed and put into another without his knowledge. ### Scholium.

<margin>The reason for the names of things.</margin>
Everything that possesses a name has received it either from its Author, or from its place [of origin], or from its resemblance [to something else], or from its change, or from its specific property. This is the reason why so many different names have been given to this matter; the reasons for these I could provide easily enough, but such an explanation would be more subtle and laborious than useful to the seeker. When the seeker has finally found and come to enjoy his "beloved" <note>A common alchemical metaphor for the successful completion of the Great Work or the discovery of the Stone.</note>, he will clearly recognize the truth of how these names were assigned. He will find that the diversity of these names does not indicate a diversity of materials, but rather the diversity of its powers according to the

*Admonitions.*

diverse names, but by studying with attention, let him meditate and be instructed—either by someone learned and of good character (if such a one can be found and known), who is not ignorant, nor a charlatan, nor a seller of recipes for third-rate hawks <note>original: "tiercelets"; likely a metaphor for inferior, fragmented, or stolen secrets.</note>, nor those "dung-healers" <note>original: "medeuins, extracteurs de merde cure"; a derogatory term for "soot-burners" or false alchemists who attempted to find the Stone in base organic waste rather than noble minerals.</note> of whatever common matter it may be, nor those who promise to draw gold, silver, and other trifles which lead only to misery, the gallows, or repentance. But more surely, let him be instructed through the diligent reading of good authors, primarily those from whom this harmony has been drawn, as well as several others who agree with them (whom I have not seen). All of these authors unanimously conclude upon a matter not yet born, but to be born by the aid of the artist, nourished by the same water that made it appear, over a small fire by a single operation, within the same vessel—that is to say, of the same form (for a small glass vessel cannot dilate or expand as the womb, the stomach, or the bladder do). And then, having found this concordance, as Travisan <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous alchemist who emphasized that the Work requires only one vessel, one furnace, and one method.</note> did, he will have cause for contentment. May God grant him this grace. Amen.

<unclear>bones</unclear>, whereby *Corfufle* <note>term: "Corfufle"; an alchemical term likely derived from Arabic, referring here to the "all-in-one" composite matter of the Stone.</note> is the entire compound, which must be burned seven times, and then it tints the whole body, and is called money, flower of copper, or of gold, or of iron, as also lead, tin, and by a thousand other names. *The Assembly of the Philosophers*, Dictum 33 <note>original: "La tourbe des Philosophes"; the *Turba Philosophorum* is one of the oldest and most famous Latin alchemical texts, written as a debate between various ancient philosophers.</note>.

"Rusting" <note>term: "Rouilleure" (Rusting); in this context, it refers to the process of oxidation or the specific stage of reddening (rubefaction) in the Great Work.</note> is a feigned and not a true name; nevertheless, I tell you that rusting is the second work, which is made from gold alone, for which reason it is called "leech," because it is hidden in the sulphur of gold, like the leech in the water. Rusting, therefore, is the whitening in the second operation; for in the first, to make the "rusting" is to whiten, in which whitening the Philosophers have commanded to put the "flower of gold," and the gold itself equally. The same, Dictum 54.

Cook the copper until the blackness comes out, which is called "money," and mix well the things of our art, and you will immediately find the blackness which is the "lead of the wise," and of which they have said many things...

### Scholium <note>term: "Scholium"; a marginal note or explanatory commentary on a difficult text.</note>.

The beginning of the work is the assembly of our gold, silver & mercury, from which a blackness is produced, named the *raven's head* <note>original: "teste de corbeau"; also known as the *caput corvi*, this signifies the *Nigredo* or "blackening" stage, representing the death and putrefaction of the initial materials before they can be reborn.</note>. In this embrace, through a mutual love heated by a fire of friendship, this hidden blackness appears—but not in a crucible or by a melting fire, as the ignorant believe. Now, this entire compound or generating mass is not the *Corfusle* <note>term: "Corfusle"; as noted previously, this refers to the "all-in-one" composite matter of the Stone.</note>, but that which is engendered, or the blackness, is the beginning of the work. This blackness must be separated from the matter, as will be shown hereafter in its proper place. Once separated, it must be watered—which he calls *burning* in order to deceive the ignorant—*seven times* (a finite number used for an indefinite one) <note>In alchemy, "seven times" often implies "repeatedly" or "as many times as necessary" to achieve the desired perfection, rather than a literal count of seven.</note>. That is to say, as many times as will be necessary to bring it to whiteness. And then it will tint any body fit to receive its tincture; for to understand "any body" plainly, this general word would exclude nothing in the world, as will be shown in the chapter on projection <note>term: "Projection"; the final stage of the Great Work where the Philosopher's Stone is cast onto a base metal to transform it into gold or silver.</note>.

He adds that even though *rusting is a feigned name*, it is nevertheless true that this "rusting" is the second work or operation, namely the blackness. For the mixing or assembly is the first operation, and this rust or blackness is called the second because, after this composition, it appears over the entire compound. He says this is made from gold alone, in the same sense that one says a man makes a child without naming the woman. And this *rusting* (he says) was hidden within the sulphur, that is to say...

It clarifies that terms like "burning" and "rusting" are metaphorical "deck-names" used to describe the purification and whitening of the matter.</summary>

...in the heat, strength, and body of the gold; just as water hides and contains the *leech* <note>The "leech" is an alchemical metaphor for a substance—often a mineral acid or mercury—that "sucks" the "blood" (the soul or essence) out of a metal.</note>. Now, this blackness suits the *leech*, as much because of the diversity of these leeches, there being some of every [shade of] black, grayish, greenish, and spotted, as also because if it has sucked and drawn too much blood, it disgorges it. Likewise, this <gloss>rust or blackness</gloss> will return the superfluity of the beverage that was given too much to it; in that he says <gloss>that the rust is the reddening at the second work</gloss> <note>term: "reddening" (rubifiement); the *Rubedo* stage, which is the final stage of the alchemical process.</note>, which is the end of the work. In this sense, he takes the black [matter] whitened by the nutrition for the first work. In this whitening, to make it fixed <note>In alchemy, "fixing" means making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note>, one must add the mercury that he calls <gloss>gold of gold</gloss>, and the silver itself which he calls <gloss>middling gold</gloss>, as will be seen hereafter in the chapter on fermentation. Finally, he exhorts the researchers to the reading and study of good books, saying that the wise have said many things within their books, as we also say.

> *Mercury is named at the beginning of the work, water; then, the blackness showing itself, earth; then being sublime <note>term: "sublime"; the process of sublimation where a solid is heated into a vapor and then recollected, signifying purification.</note>, air; being red, fire; for the gold must be cooked by itself until redness, then sublime with some luminous body or leaven, then added alone to the whole and to the corruption of the matter, spirit; and thus the earth with the water, the air, and the fire is one body, the leaven, the soul.*

### Scholium.

The beginning of the work is the assembly of our gold, silver, and mercury, from which the blackness is produced—named the *raven’s head* <note>original: *teste de corbeau*; this refers to the *Nigredo* stage, the first essential phase of the alchemical process representing decomposition and the "death" of the metals.</note>—within this *embrace* by a mutual love heated by a fire of friendship <note>A "fire of friendship" refers to a gentle, steady heat, often compared to the warmth of a brooding hen or a water bath, rather than the violent heat of a forge.</note>. This hidden blackness appears, but not in a crucible or by a melting fire, as the ignorant believe. Now, this entire compound or engendering mass is not the *Corfusle* <note>term: "Corfusle"; a specific name for the alchemical matter used in the *Turba Philosophorum*, often identifying the "body" of the substance before its final transformation.</note>, but that which is engendered <insert>or the blackness is the beginning of the work, which must be separated from the matter as will be shown hereafter in its proper place; and being separated, it must be watered—which he calls *burning* to deceive the ignorant—*seven times* (a finite number used for an indefinite one), that is to say, as many times as necessary to bring it to whiteness. Then, it will tint every body fit to receive its tincture; for to understand "every body" literally would exclude nothing in the world, as will be shown in the chapter on projection <note>The final stage of the work where the Philosopher's Stone is cast upon base metals to transform them into gold.</note>.</insert> He adds that even though *rustiness* <note>original: *rouilleure*</note> is a feigned name, nonetheless this "rust-like" substance is the second work or operation, namely the blackness. For the mixing or assembly is the first operation, and this rust or blackness is called the second because, after this composition, it appears over the whole compound. He says this is made from gold alone, in the same sense that one says a man makes a child without naming the woman; and this *rustiness* (he says) was hidden within the sulfur, that is to say

# CHAPTER I.
E iiij

<unclear>In</unclear> the heat, strength, and body of the gold; just as water hides and contains the *leech*: now this blackness is appropriate for the *leech*, as much because of the diversity among them—there being some that are entirely black, some grayish, some greenish, and some spotted—as also because if it has sucked and drawn too much blood, it disgorges itself. In the same way, this *rustiness* or *blackness* <note>In alchemy, "rust" often refers to the surface oxidation or the initial impurity that must be transformed. Here, the author uses the "leech" analogy to describe how the matter absorbs and then releases its "blood" or essence.</note> will return the surplus of the drink that was given to it in excess. In that which he says, that the *rust is the rubification at the second work*, this belongs to the end of the work; and in this sense, he takes the blackness whitened by nutrition <note>term: nutrition; the process of "feeding" the alchemical matter with "mercurial water" to help it grow or transition between stages.</note> to be the first work. To make this whitening fixed <note>term: fixed; a substance that can withstand high heat without evaporating or changing.</note>, one must add the mercury which he calls the *flower of gold*, and even the silver which he calls *gold itself* <note>original: "or mesme"; alchemists often used "gold" and "silver" interchangeably or in coded ways to refer to their perfected "Sulfur" and "Mercury."</note>, as will be seen hereafter in the chapter on fermentation. Finally, he exhorts researchers to the reading and study of good books, saying that the sages have said many things within their books, just as we also say.

> *Mercury is named at the beginning of the work, water; then, when the blackness shows itself, earth; then, being sublimated <note>term: sublimated; the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again, used here to represent the "Air" stage.</note>, air; being red, fire; for gold must be cooked by itself until redness, then sublimated with some luminous body or leaven <note>term: leaven; a substance added to the "dead" matter to give it "life" and trigger transformation, much like yeast in bread.</note>, then added alone to the whole and to the corruption of the matter, spirit; and thus the earth with the water, the air, and the fire is one body; the leaven is the soul.*

## CHEMICAL HARMONY
<meta>catchword: the o-</meta>

...and the final sprinkling <note>original: *irroration*; the alchemical process of moistening a dry substance with a liquid, often compared to dew falling on grass.</note>, it is the spirit of mercury alone. In the Ninth Exercise of the Tomb <note>Likely a reference to the 9th Dictum of the *Turba Philosophorum* (The Assembly of the Philosophers); "tombe" was occasionally used as a corruption of "turbe" in early French manuscripts.</note>.

->Scholium.<-

By saying *the air must be cooked*, etc., he means that when the blackness is whitened—which he calls *air*—one must neither feed it further nor ferment it to lead it toward the red color, but merely continue the fire. Once this red color arrives—which he calls *sublimation* <note>In this context, sublimation refers to the "exaltation" or spiritualization of the matter as it reaches its highest state, rather than just the physical transition from solid to gas.</note>—it will be mixed with *its luminous body*. This body is the Moon <note>Silver or the "white" stage of the work.</note> if the matter is left at the white, or the Sun <note>Gold or the "red" stage of the work.</note> if the matter is red. These two luminaries are called *leaven or fermentation*; and when the whole is completed, it is called *spirit*, because by its subtlety it penetrates the metals with which it is composed. From two things differing in nature—namely, a subtle one and a coarse one—an entirely different whole is created.

### TEXT.

<margin>Rosinus <note>The Latinized name for Zosimos of Panopolis, a foundational Greco-Egyptian alchemist of the 3rd century.</note></margin>
The foliated earth <note>term: foliated earth (*terra foliata*); a specific state of the alchemical matter where it appears to have a flaky or leaf-like structure.</note>, or the earth of leaves, meaning "of garments": for leaves are the covering of trees, and our water is the garment of our aquatic earth. Rosinus, p. 297.

<meta>This page continues the commentary on alchemical terminology, specifically following the previous discussion on the transition from white to red stages. It clarifies a cryptic term mentioned in the preceding sections attributed to "the Scot" (Michael Scot).</meta>

# CHAPTER I.
<meta>catchword: of the Scot.</meta>

<margin>
...division remains in you inseparably, namely through Wisdom. The same author, p. 312.
</margin>

->Scholium.<-

This term **foliated earth** <note>original: *terre feuillee*; also known in Latin as *terra foliata*, it refers to the purified alchemical earth or the "salt" of the matter when it begins to crystallize into leaf-like flakes.</note> has caused great trouble for thousands of people. Some take it to mean bricks or tiles, others metals or ores, others the leaves and bark of trees; and without wishing to open their eyes, they throw themselves into inextricable confusion. 

Now, the Sages called the **small furnace** they used "foliated earth," because just as leaves protect fruit from external injury, so too is their matter preserved by and within it. Trevisan <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous Italian alchemist.</note> says in this regard that the fountain (which is the entirety of the matter within the matrass <note>A long-necked glass flask used for digestion and distillation.</note>) had been placed in a "hollow of oak," which is a wooden box. Another calls the matrass "foliated earth" for the same reason, and another calls mercury "foliated earth" because it envelops and covers all the metals from which the blackness <note>The *nigredo* or black stage, the initial phase of decomposition in the Great Work.</note> emerges. 

And others—indeed the majority—take the blackness floating atop the compound to be the "foliated earth," which, like a garment or mask, covers with its ugliness the most beautiful thing we need, which is the **white** or the **red** <note>The *albedo* (white) and *rubedo* (red) stages representing the purification and final perfection of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. This is clearly indicated by this author. As for the saying that Adam brought it from Paradise, and that "man is its mine" <note>An alchemical aphorism suggesting that the raw materials for the Stone are found within the human being or are common to all.</note>, this was discussed in the previous scholium on the text of the Scot <note>Likely Michael Scot (c. 1175–1232), a scholar and translator whose name was often associated with medieval alchemical treatises.</note>.

<summary>This page explains the various metaphorical interpretations of the alchemical term "foliated earth," clarifying that it refers to the protective environment of the work or the initial "black" stage of the matter.</summary>
<keywords>foliated earth, terra foliata, Bernard of Trevisan, matrass, mercury, nigredo, Michael Scot, alchemical furnace</keywords>

-># CHEMICAL HARMONY<-

...pure substance and branches, those sensible things in others, and like unto them: Among these compounds, there are some which, it is believed, did not begin from a seed, but truly they grow and live secretly, multiply and are born. And what prevents them (namely the metals) from producing their like, from engendering, and from being able to convert [other substances] into themselves, is that their **vivifying spirit** is submerged and hindered by too much matter. This spirit is what Art <note>In this context, "Art" refers to Alchemy—the human skill used to perfect or accelerate natural processes.</note> can extract, it being certain that since every soul is incorporated, and that the whole world and its parts have bodies, there must be a middle spirit between these two things, which can be called neither soul nor body, but partakes of both; and it is this spirit that desires the multiplication of that in which it resides.

Now, gold among all metals is abundant in this spirit, which our Author names the ***incombustible grain, radical moisture, and seed***. This seed multiplies marvelously in the mine, and in each according to its property, sooner or later, Nature working there incessantly both by night and by day, which he calls here **Sun and Moon**. But if the learned Artist draws this **seed**—called by many **sulfur** <note>Alchemical sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle within a substance, not necessarily the chemical element sulfur.</note>—from it and joins it with its **glaire** <note>original: *glaire*; literally "egg white." In alchemy, this refers to the viscous, clear mercurial fluid that acts as the "womb" or medium for the sulfurous seed.</note>, he will have in a short time what nature cannot do alone in several hundred years; always remembering that this work is natural, and therefore one must proceed gently, for nature abhors violence.

<note>This passage argues that metals are not "dead" matter but possess a hidden life force or "seed" that allows them to grow in the earth. The alchemist's role is to remove the "excess matter" that stifles this growth, allowing the metal to reproduce or "multiply" rapidly in the laboratory.</note>

**metals, vivifying spirit, incombustible grain, radical moisture, seed, mine, Sun, Moon, sulfur, glaire, nature**

<summary>This page explains that metals possess a hidden 'vivifying spirit' or 'seed' which is suppressed by their physical density. It describes how an alchemist can extract this seed (sulfur) and combine it with a viscous medium (glaire) to accelerate the natural growth of gold.</summary>
<keywords>vivifying spirit, radical moisture, alchemical sulfur, glaire, growth of metals, natural philosophy, transmutation, vitalism</keywords>

This will not be accepted by everyone, but it suffices for me that one among a thousand understands what is treated herein.

->Text.<-

Sulfur is called the father or agent of minerals, and quicksilver <term>quicksilver: mercury; here referring to the "philosophical" mercury which represents the passive or feminine principle</term> is the mother, or patient <note>patient: in traditional philosophy and alchemy, the "patient" is the substance that receives the action or influence of the "agent"</note>. The Philosophers <term>Philosophers: a term alchemists used for themselves, meaning "lovers of wisdom" or practitioners of the "Natural Philosophy"</term> have sometimes named the stone in their books the "elixir," and sometimes the "stone," making no great distinction between the two, in order to deceive or astonish fools; and yet they have spoken the truth, insofar as the elixir is the true principle, the main foundation and root of the Philosophers' precious stone. The same [author] ch. 5, 12, book 2. The composition that we make with our own hands has no proper name, which is the reason why it is called the "blessed stone," even though it is not a stone, nor does it have the nature of a stone. Some have named it a mineral, vegetable, animal, and mental stone: [mineral] because the things from which it is composed are less than minerals in their nature; and it is called "vegetable" because it turns green and grows like plants; "animal" because it has a body, soul, and spirit, like animals. Some have said that it is vile and stinking, because its "womb" <note>original: "ventre"; refers to the interior of the alchemical vessel during the decomposition stage</note> is full of a foul odor when one composes it; and others have said that it was a "chaos," or the "origin of the world," or a "confused mass," and a—

*...[it has] been called by several other names by the Philosophers, but we call it red earth. The same [author], book 1, chapter 10.*

*The red earth is the Sun <note>Sun: In the symbolic language of alchemy, the Sun (Sol) represents gold or the perfected masculine principle of Sulfur.</note>. The same, book 1, chapter 17.*

*The man having a helmet on his head is the cucurbit <term>cucurbit: the lower, gourd-shaped vessel of a distillation apparatus, often called a "body."</term>, and the bottom of it is the belly or the feet; this is the reason why it is said that the matter is dung, which men trample underfoot—that is to say, the man with the helmet—and it is thrown onto the paths, meaning before the Philosophers in this science, upon which paths it seems to be cast away, meaning as if dead until it is revived. The same, book 2, chapter 46.*

*Thelesme <note>original: "Thelesme"; derived from the Greek "thelema" (will or power) as found in the Emerald Tablet of Hermes Trismegistus, here interpreted as a "treasure" or "secret power."</note> means secret or treasure. The same, chapter 17.*

*When the Philosophers speak of joining sal ammoniac <term>sal ammoniac: ammonium chloride; in alchemy, it represents a volatile spirit used to "animate" or "open" the fixed bodies of metals.</term> with the body and the spirit, they mean the air drawn from mercury, that is to say, our water or our volatile spirit or the dragon's tail <note>dragon's tail: often refers to the residue or the volatile part of the matter that must be controlled; here it signifies the "spirit" of the substance.</note>. The same, book 1, chapter 20.*

*Hibar <note>original: "Hibar"; likely a corruption of the Arabic word "hibār" (ink or dye), used in alchemy to signify a substance with coloring power.</note> means a staining medicine <term>staining medicine: also known as a "tincture," a substance capable of penetrating and changing the color and quality of a metal.</term>. The same, chapter 22.*

*Nature is only improved within its own nature, just as you are only improved in your son <note>This emphasizes the alchemical principle that the "Stone" must be born from its own kind—gold from gold, spirit from spirit—modeled on human generation.</note>. The same, chapter 25.*

<summary>This page provides a series of definitions for alchemical metaphors, explaining how physical apparatus and chemicals (like the cucurbit and sal ammoniac) correspond to symbolic concepts like the "man with a helmet" and the "dragon's tail." It reinforces the idea that the alchemical process is a natural generation, likened to a father being improved through his son.</summary>

### CHAPTER I.

Gold is mineral sulfur, possessing a very good odor to his wife—that is to say, to silver <note>In the symbolic "Chemical Wedding" of alchemy, Gold represents the masculine principle (Sulfur/King) and Silver represents the feminine principle (Mercury/Queen). ->Scholium.<-

Many have been extremely troubled to find out what this <term>Elixir</term> <note>Elixir: from the Arabic "al-iksir," referring to a medicinal or transformative substance capable of changing base metals into gold or healing the human body.</note> is. Some, believing it to be something simple, have leafed through the greater part of dictionaries and interpreters of foreign languages; having worked upon various drugs, they found nothing whatsoever there to satisfy them. Others have believed it to be some compound, and having wished to practice all the recipes of <term>Geber</term> <note>Geber: The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a legendary 8th-century scholar considered the father of chemistry.</note>, of <term>Lull</term> <note>Lull: Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a philosopher whose name was attached to many later alchemical texts.</note>, of <term>Archelaus</term>, of <term>Rupescissa</term> <note>Jean de Roquetaillade: A 14th-century friar famous for his work on "quintessence" or the distilled spirit of wine.</note>, and of several others, they found nothing in the end but wind. Deceived in their fantastical researches, they finally chose to follow nothing but their own imaginations—because, they say, one would have to be a diviner to learn of the Stone and its matter through the variety of names found in all the authors, who only agree in deceiving men.

Leaving aside then these owls and <term>shunners of light</term> <note>original: "lucifuges"; a term for those who avoid the light of truth, comparing them to nocturnal animals.</note>, I say in good conscience that I know nothing of this science except through the reading of books, to which we have joined experience; this collection, or harmony, will serve us as an irreproachable witness to this. Now, I have not yet been able to learn from any author who first made use of this word <term>Elixir</term>, which may proceed from the Latin verb **to draw out** <note>original: "elicio"; meaning to entice, evoke, or draw forth.</note>, which signifies to draw out, because one draws out from the...

matters, from which one extracts what was hidden within them, namely the black matter; or from the verb *to boil* <note>original Latin: "elixo," meaning to boil thoroughly or cook</note>, which means to cook or to boil, inasmuch as that which is drawn from the Sun and Moon <note>Gold and Silver</note> is cooked and as if boiled, namely within the mercury <note>Quicksilver, used here as the "solvent" or medium for the reaction</note>. And through this cooking, that which was black becomes white, then yellow, then red <note>These colors represent the standard progression of the "Great Work": Nigredo (black), Albedo (white), Citrinitas (yellow), and Rubedo (red).</note>. If one prefers it to be an Arabic name, which some interpret as quintessence <note>The "fifth essence," a pure, spiritual substance beyond the four classical elements.</note>, I do not object; nevertheless, it is a name that designates nothing other than a subtle matter drawn from a coarser one, being taken and understood by some for the blackness, by others for the whiteness, and by others for the redness—for some have said "white elixir," and others have said "red elixir."

But our Author, by *elixir*, has without difficulty understood the blackness, insofar as he says that it is the *foundation of the stone*; and the completed work is the precious stone and blessed stone of the Philosophers, which, he says, *turns green and grows* in the manner of vegetables <note>In alchemy, minerals were thought to "grow" or "vegetate" within the earth like plants.</note>, being watered by its own water, increasing by apposition <note>Growth by adding new layers of matter to the exterior.</note> in both quantity and quality. Regarding his statement that nothing must be added to gold and silver, which he names husband and wife, the response is that the male and the female produce their seed from themselves; but sometimes this seed is so thick that if it were not made fluid by "Medicine," no generation could follow from it. Likewise, if the learned Artist did not add its own humidity to these two bodies—I say "own," for otherwise their seed or sulfur <note>The active, fiery principle within the metal.</note> would never exit from them, and therefore one would not have what is desired.

IF anyone finds that I have brought forward too many authors to prove and teach that one must not pay regard to so many names, let him know that I have not written this treatise for him; but if, on the contrary, this treatise falls into the hands of a lover of the truth, of which he desires to see the nakedness, and possessing a good soul he is desirous of embracing it, I pray him to believe that I have more authors than I have cited here, who all cry out unanimously that the purification of imperfect metals <note>In alchemical theory, base metals like lead or iron were considered "imperfect" versions of gold and silver, "diseased" by impurities.</note>, and so named (with regard to gold and silver) is done by what is called the Stone of the Philosophers, which has its...

# CHAPTER I.

[for the] beginning of astralized gold and quicksilver <note>original: "or & de l'argent vif astralise." Quicksilver is mercury. "Astralized" refers to metals that have been purified or "exalted" to align with celestial or "astral" influences.</note>, from which two the seed, or sulfur, or germ, is drawn in black form and color; a volatile thing nourished by its own milk <note>original: "propre laict." In alchemy, "Virgin's Milk" (lac virginis) often refers to a mercurial solvent or restorative liquid used during the whitening stage.</note> until it has acquired the white color, then the yellow, and then the red <note>These colors represent the classic stages of the alchemical Great Work: Nigredo (black), Albedo (white), Citrinitas (yellow), and Rubedo (red).</note>; and to one of these two: ally it with its own body, or the ferment, or the soul through the intervention of its own spirit, and all within a single vessel—that is to say, in one form—by a single fire, without worrying about the diversity and plurality of names that have been given to it, whether by likeness, or by property, or to deceive the over-confident <note>original: "trop cuides," referring to those who are excessively self-assured or arrogant in their own false knowledge.</note>. For it is permitted, in order to properly prove a fact and a truth, and to baffle both the lie and the liars, to hear many irreproachable witnesses; for, as a great Scholar says, *Where there is consent among the learned in the matter inquired into, there is the truth, which needs neither makeup nor external ornaments, being more beautiful and more desirable completely naked than coiffed and masked; upon which a fine wit of our time has said:*

> He who would see you, Truth, completely naked,
> Oh, how ardently he would be enamored of you,
> Seeing that at all times the rarest spirits
> Make love to you through a cloud. <note>In this poetic context, "making love" refers to intellectual pursuit or devotion, while the "cloud" represents the veils of allegory, metaphor, and "decked-out" language that often hide the truth.</note>

Since we have marked the reason for so many names, and tried to unseal the eyes of the blind and open the ears of the deaf, and uncovered the distractions of charlatans <note>original: "charlatans," a term frequently used by serious alchemists to describe "puffers" or fraudulent practitioners who deceived the public with false promises.</note>, let us lead the strayed back to the straight and unique path, showing that there is only one by which one can acquire this much-sought-after good.

# TO KNOW WHETHER THERE ARE
## SEVERAL WAYS TO

->CHAPTER II.<-

->*TEXT.*<-

The entire work is completed by a single way, by a single thing, by a single disposition, and by a single action.
> In the book entitled the Lily <note>original: "Lilium." This likely refers to the *Lilium inter spinas* (The Lily among Thorns), a popular alchemical treatise attributed to various authors including Alain de Lille or Bernard of Trevisan.</note>.

# CHAPTER II.

...[we] diminish, except that we remove the superfluous things in its preparation. Nicolas des Comptes <note>Nicolas des Comptes (Nicolaus de Comitibus) was a 16th-century alchemist known for his "Six Treatises" which synthesized earlier medieval traditions.</note>, page 1.

This may be a shorthand for "tropa" or a specific name for the Stone in its elevated state.</note> or superior stone, and that thing is born from our mercury alone; for it overcomes everything and is not overcome by it, but enjoying its company, it remains there amiably. The same [author], page 2.

All science consists of a single thing, which creates all perfection when it is prepared by our artifice; and this preparation is done with subtle judgment and great prudence, and all perfection depends on the **regime of the fire** <term>regime of the fire: the specific sequence and intensity of heat (often described as "grades") applied to the alchemical vessel over time.</term>, and therein lies the whole secret; our art does not lie in the variety and multitude of things, and that is the truth. The same [author], page 3.

From a single matter of well-purified metals, one has the white and red sulfur; therefore, let us prepare the bodies well—namely gold and silver—to the end that we may have from them the sulfur and the **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: mercury (argent vif), representing the volatile, feminine principle in alchemy which must be joined with the masculine sulfur.</term>; let he who has ears, let him hear <note>original: "qui a oreille pour soye." An archaic or phonetic variation of the biblical "he who has ears to hear."</note>. The same [author], page 5.

### CHAPTER II.

...we diminish, except that we remove the superfluous things in its preparation. Nicolas des Comtes <note>Nicolaus de Comitibus, a late medieval writer on the "metallic transformatory art."</note>, p. 1.

I tell you that true science is accustomed to being made from a single thing without adding or diminishing, and this thing is called **Adrop** <term>Adrop: A Persian-derived term used by alchemists to refer to the "body" or raw matter of the Stone, often identified with lead or the "Philosophical Saturn."</term> or the superior stone, and this thing is made from our **mercury** <term>mercury: Not the common metal, but "Philosophical Mercury," the refined spirit or solvent believed to be the "mother" of all metals.</term> alone, for it overcomes the fire and is not overcome by it, but rejoicing with it, it remains there in friendship. The same, p. 2.

All science consists in a single thing, which creates all perfection when it is prepared by our artifice; and this preparation is done with a subtle judgment and great prudence, and all perfection depends on the **regime of the fire** <term>regime: the specific management of heat (intensity and duration) which dictates the various stages of the alchemical transformation.</term>, and therein lies the whole secret, for our art does not consist in the variety and multitude of things, and this is true. The same, p. 3.

<image-desc>In the right margin, there are four vertical wavy lines drawn in ink, resembling flames or rising smoke.</image-desc>

The same, p. 5.

->**CHEMICAL HARMONY**<-

...to which we add nothing foreign, nor do we diminish, except for the superfluous things, which we remove during the preparation; for nothing enters into it which has not come out of it, either in whole or in part, for if one adds something foreign to it, immediately it is corrupted, and what one seeks to do is not done. Dastin <note>John Dastin (c. 1290–1350) was a famous English alchemist known for his letters to Pope John XXII defending the possibility of transmutation.</note>, p. 28.

The white and red **Medicines** <term>Medicines: In alchemy, the "Medicine" is another name for the Philosopher's Stone, capable of "curing" imperfect metals into gold or silver.</term> are not different from each other, except that the red medicine requires a greater **sublimation** <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it becomes a vapor and then solidifying it again to purify it.</term>, a longer **digestion** <term>digestion: a stage where the substance is kept under gentle, constant heat, similar to how food is processed in a stomach.</term>, and a hotter fire. The same [Dastin], p. 29.

<margin>William of Paris <note>Guillaume Pariſien: This likely refers to William of Auvergne (c. 1180–1249), a Bishop of Paris to whom several alchemical treatises were apocryphally attributed.</note></margin>
Although the Philosophers divide their **mastery** <term>mastery: (French: magiſtere) the entire chemical procedure or the resulting Philosopher's Stone itself.</term> into several operations, truly there is but a single one and a single means of operating: namely, to take the water of the earth and return the water onto the earth until the earth rots and purifies itself, so much so that it dissolves afterwards and becomes entirely spiritual with the spirit; and this is then called the gold and **quicksilver of the Philosophers** <term>quicksilver: Not common mercury, but a "living" or "philosophical" mercury believed to be the essential spirit of all metals.</term>; then the entire body is resolved into spirit. William of Paris, beginning [the work] *My Lord, under Correction*, p. 46.

One must **redden** <note>original: "rougir"—referring to the Rubedo or "Reddening," the final stage of the Great Work which produces the Stone for transmuting gold.</note> [the matter] in the same fashion, and in the same place, and in the same vessel, or one similar, and with the same weight of water and similar **imbibitions** <term>imbibitions: the process of repeatedly adding liquid to a dry substance until it is completely saturated or "nourished."</term>.

## CHAPTER II.

...waterings, proportions, and the same colors, and finally to observe all the same **regimens** <term>regimens: the specific stages of heating and processing required to reach a certain alchemical goal.</term> as with the white stage. Florent. <note>This likely refers to a commentator or author found in alchemical compendiums; possibly a reference to a specific chapter in the works of Robert Fludd or a similar author known as "Florentius."</note> Book 2, chapter 17.

There is but a single **medicine** <term>medicine: in alchemy, another name for the Philosopher's Stone, which was thought to "cure" base metals of their impurities.</term>, and it is called "single" because it alone removes that which is imperfect, soiled, and mixed within metals. Now, every other medicine, however good and **fixed** <term>fixed: a term for a substance that is stable under heat and does not evaporate or escape the vessel.</term> it may be, removes nothing imperfect nor soiled from the metals, but only palliates and covers it; for this reason, they are called **sophistical** <term>sophistical: referring to "false" or "deceptive" processes that create a superficial appearance of gold or silver without truly changing the metal's essence.</term>. The same [Florent.], Book 2, chapter 25.

**Geber** <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815). However, the "Great Perfection" mentioned here refers to the *Summa perfectionis magisterii*, a highly influential 13th-century Latin work attributed to him by later scholars.</note>, in his *Great Perfection of the Mastery*, maintains that there is only one unique medicine, so much so that it clearly appears to those who look closely that under a great mass of sophistical operations, he speaks of, hides, and intends that one alone. And in the same way that he hides it from the "sons" <note>original: "fils"; a reference to the "Sons of Art" or "Sons of Wisdom," the traditional name for initiated students of alchemy.</note>, he reveals it to the wise who understand it well. And since all the medicines found in his book on the *Great Perfection* are sophistical, they must be left aside—as is said in the same chapter—without caring for them, just as Geber did not care for them in the operation of the Great Medicine. The same, Book 2, chapter 25.

Every **physical stone** <term>physical stone: a synonym for the Philosopher’s Stone, emphasizing its material existence as a product of natural "physics" or chemistry.</term> is mineral, even though it takes its origin from three natures, and though the Philosophers seem...

While studying, I looked curiously for where the books agreed best, for I knew very well that the truth was in the books, and that where they agree is where we must hold fast, and I believed the truth to be there: since one can only speak the truth in one single thing: for, as I have said, where many agree, one must believe that the truth is there, even if one names it in one way and another in another; however, the deception lies in the diversity, and not in the agreement, and to show that this is true they set forth only a single means although they write it in various ways and figures. Trevisan <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous alchemist who emphasized that the "multiplicity" of descriptions in alchemical literature usually hides a single, simple truth.</note> in book 2 of his opuscule.

# CHEMICAL HARMONY

[the] matter should be placed in a clean vessel, and cooked by a philosophical fire <note>A "philosophical fire" refers to a steady, controlled heat—often compared to the warmth of a brooding hen—rather than a literal roaring flame.</note> without intermission. Reuillach <note>Likely referring to Jean de Roquetaillade, also known as Johannes de Rupescissa, a 14th-century alchemist.</note>, page 5.

An Elixir is a compound medicine, metallic, perfected from sulfur and quicksilver <note>original: "argent vif"; mercury.</note>, joined inseparably by fire. The same [author], page 9.

It seems to me that I have sufficiently shown that there is only one single path to obtain and perfect the physical stone <note>The "physical stone" is another term for the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. The same [author], page 28.

<margin>Synesius</margin>
Although the Philosophers have spoken differently according to the letter <note>That is, according to the literal wording of their texts.</note>, nonetheless they all agree, and show the same path, which is the white and red stone <note>The "white stone" transforms metals into silver; the "red stone" (the final goal) transforms them into gold.</note>, which is made from the same root, and by the same order. CHAPTER II.

...the medicine, but composed of several things, which does not change in the fire, but rather mixes with the metals when they are melted without being burned; this medicine cannot be made in a short time, whereby he who will not have patience should not set himself to work, because believing himself to be advancing by hurrying, he will spoil everything. ### Notice from the Scholiast <note>A scholiast is a scholar who writes explanatory notes or commentaries on a text.</note>.

I did not wish to dwell on giving a clearer understanding of any of the passages cited in this second chapter, since we are about to enter another, in which we will clarify many things; it is enough to have shown that the *Philosophers* <note>The ancient masters of alchemy.</note> never intended that there were different ways to reach the desired goal. Therefore, let all those who say that—just as there are several ways to go to Paris, Rome, and other places, there are likewise several ways to attain this <term>science</term> <note>Specifically the "Divine Science" of alchemy, the art of transmutation.</note>—know that they are very heavily mistaken. I grant that from several places and parts of the world one can come to Paris or to Rome: but they must grant me that there is only one single gate to enter within. From all places on earth one can go to heaven, but there is only one single entrance, of which our only Savior Jesus Christ says that He is the door, the truth, and the life <note>A paraphrase of John 14:6: "I am the way, the truth, and the life." The author is using a religious analogy to insist that there is only one true method in alchemy.</note>,

<summary>This page concludes the argument that alchemy requires immense patience and follows only one true path, as evidenced by the writings of Geber. The commentator (Scholiast) rejects the idea that there are multiple ways to achieve the Great Work, using the analogy of a single gate to a city or to heaven.</summary>

<keywords>Geber, Magistery, patience in alchemy, unity of the work, the one gate, Scholiast, Paris, Rome, Jesus Christ</keywords>

->HARM. CHYM. CH. II.<-

...and that no one can go to the Father except through him: <note>Referring to the previous page's quote of John 14:6: "No man cometh unto the Father, but by me." The author uses this to argue that just as there is only one way to salvation, there is only one way to the Great Work.</note> one might object to me that Mirandulanus <note>Giovanni Francesco Pico della Mirandola (1469–1533), an Italian philosopher and author of *De Auro* (On Gold), a famous Renaissance text on alchemy.</note> writes that he saw a man who had more than twenty ways of making gold. Therefore, one must conclude [that there are many ways]. I reply that this conclusion is foolish, for the one of whom he speaks could mix his powder or "little stone" <note>The Philosopher's Stone.</note> with alum, sulfur, wax, tallow, soap, and other such materials, which vanished into mixed smoke when thrown upon the metals—whether already melted or about to be melted—and only the **staining** and **fixed Medicine** <note>The "Tincture." Alchemists believed the Stone acted as a "Medicine" that cured "sick" (base) metals by staining them with the color and permanence of gold.</note> remained with these metals, which it colored and fixed. But if Mirandulanus had said he had seen an infinity of entirely different materials from which, or by which, the metals were transmuted, that would be another matter; but he does not say so. And even if it were so, his authority would have no force against so many weighty Authors. Let us then cut it short and say with the *Philosophers* <note>The traditional masters of the alchemical art.</note> that there is **only one path, one matter, one vessel, and one fire**; and all those who seek more, or seek others, are deluding themselves and deceiving those who believe them. Like the blind, both the leader and the led will fall into the pit <note>original: "precipice"</note> of regret, and perhaps of shame. As for us, we shall be content to follow and show the desired path, which will reveal to us the necessary materials.
