### Scholium. <note>A "Scholium" is a marginal note or explanatory commentary on a text, common in scholarly and scientific books of this period.</note>

Several difficulties surround me, and I fear that—much like the Phrygians <note>The Phrygians were ancient people of Asia Minor, famous in antiquity for their intricate and colorful embroidery.</note> who weave a tapestry of a thousand colors pleasing to many and displeasing to one, and after having gathered the sweetest of every kind of flower like the bee—some scatterbrain may suck out a marrow which serves him as a <term>Sejan horse</term> <note>original: "cheual Seian"; a reference to the "Equus Seianus," a legendary horse that supposedly brought ruin to every person who owned it. # CHAPTER III.

<unclear>what</unclear> past they could only inquire after [for a] <unclear>re</unclear>certain time after which they could discuss. I have not yet learned, as is proper, and consequently practiced the doctrine of **Strabo** <note>Strabo (c. 64 BC – 24 AD) was a Greek geographer and philosopher. The author here attributes to him a theory of physiognomy—the art of judging character from physical appearance.</note>, who, locating the rational soul in the eyebrows, said that if the hairs were straight, one was a fool; if they leaned toward the nose, a buffoon; if toward the temples, a mocker; and if completely cast down, envious.

But I would very much desire that those who cast themselves into this research should have three guides in heaven, three in the soul, and three on the earth: **Mercury** for the search, **Phoebus** <note>A name for Apollo, the sun god, here representing intellectual light or illumination.</note> for light, and **Venus** for grace; an ardent and stable will, a subtle mind, and a good memory; a prudent father of the family, a highly approved preceptor, and a most learned physician. This is to the end that, if one were to catch a plague, he might not serve as a <term>Pharmakos</term> <note>original: "Pharmaque"; in ancient Greece, a "Pharmakos" was a human scapegoat, often a criminal or a slave, who was ritualistically fed, struck, and sometimes sacrificed or expelled to purify a city from disaster.</note> holding in one hand a cheese, a cake, and wild figs; and after having been struck seven times, he should not be burned alive and his ashes cast away for the salvation of the people. 

But I know not if my dream shall be theoretical or allegorical; nonetheless, if during the <term>Halcyon days</term> <note>original: "iours Alcynoides"; a mythical period of calm weather during midwinter, supposed to occur when the halcyon bird (kingfisher) was nesting.</note> we do not arrive at the harbor, perhaps some favorable wind will push us there. Let us then take to the oar and venture into this **mercury**, navigating within it to discover the best havens, the most difficult reefs, and the deepest abysses. Let us mark for those who have eyes and ears the shipwreck that a thousand people have made and continue to make daily, for lack of knowing how to recognize the <term>lodestone</term> <note>original: "roche d’aymant"; a naturally magnetic rock. In medieval and Renaissance lore, "magnetic mountains" were feared by sailors because they could pull the iron nails right out of a ship’s hull.</note> against which their iron-shod vessel imprudently hurls itself.

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the philosophical requirements of the seeker (Chapter III) into the specific chemical theory of the "Chemical Harmony." The author is now defining the relationship between Mercury and the noble metals.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>CHEMICAL HARMONY</header>

If all things are made of the matter to which they are reduced, there is no difficulty in accepting that metals are made of mercury. This is the reason why **Thomas** <note>Likely referring to Pseudo-Thomas Aquinas, the name under which several influential alchemical treatises were written in the Middle Ages.</note> along with the other **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to alchemists, the "Philosophers of Fire."</note> tells us that mercury alone completes our work; this is to exclude any other matter that is corrosive or destructive to metals. Mercury, therefore, truly dissolves the **Sun** <note>Alchemical term for Gold.</note> and the **Moon** <note>Alchemical term for Silver.</note>. I say "dissolves," which is to say it draws or pulls out from their bodies, by means of the **Artist's** <note>The practitioner or alchemist performing the work.</note> help, a matter which is neither Sun nor Moon, and can never be so.

Just as from the male and the female comes a matter or sperm which is neither male nor female, and can never be that specific man or woman from whom the matter issued—but rather something resembling one or the other—likewise, that which comes out of this Sun and Moon by means of mercury, aided by a suitable heat, will never be and cannot be (whatever artifice one applies to it) Sun and Moon. This happens without any loss to the quality of the Sun; the Sun and Moon find themselves diminished only by the weight of that extracted matter. 

Let those, therefore, who wish to practice this admirable work learn that our **dissolution** is not the kind understood by those who merely compile recipes. Let them also learn to understand our words and terms, without which they will never be able to properly understand the intention of the Philosophers who have written expressly regarding this science. Now, says our Author, the gold and silver—which are called "domestics," the brother and sister of the said mercury—must be dissolved, that is to say re-

returned to its primary nature, which is volatile. To ensure that one does not believe he speaks simply of common mercury, he adds that those who understand it this way are mistaken, saying that one must work not only with mercury but with the gold and silver mixed into it. From their composition, one will draw the *virgin’s milk* <term>Virgin's Milk: A symbolic term for a white liquid or emulsion produced during alchemical operations, often associated with the dissolution of the "stone" in its early stages.</term>, which is called by an infinity of names. This is that *blackness* <note>The *Nigredo* stage, or "black of blacks," which represents the death and putrefaction of the material before it can be purified.</note>, which has such power that it stifles, even in its cradle, an incredible quantity of pure mercury. I believe we would not be wrong to take it for that *Hercules* who killed, while still in swaddling clothes, *that great and horrible serpent*; just as it is *that ungrateful child who kills its mother* <note>A classic alchemical metaphor: the refined substance (the child) is born from and eventually consumes or transforms the raw matter (the mother) from which it originated.</note>, for this impalpable and volatile black powder, when guided wisely, returns common mercury to its own black and impalpable nature. 

Now, I marvel at so many seekers who, like blind moles following their own fantasies, understand "mercury" to mean an infinity of corrosive waters, which they call solvents, *menstruums* <term>Menstruum: From the Latin *menstruus* (monthly); in alchemy, this refers to a solvent with the power to dissolve solids and "animate" the matter, similar to how blood was thought to sustain an embryo.</term>, and similar nonsense. They compose these with much toil, peril, and expense, or else they amuse and delude themselves by trying to extract mercury from metals. Through a lack of understanding of what they are meddling with, they plunge into a sea of anguish, from which they emerge only with the loss of their wealth, their honor, <margin>*Four mercuries.*</margin> and sometimes both. Let them therefore seek attentively, and learn at the expense of others. We have *four mercuries* besides *two others*, of which we shall speak shortly. The first is the common kind which truly dissolves (once it is properly cleaned) the two others

<header>CHAPTER III.</header>

...distilled, until you can paint with it; then dry them by a small fire. The same author, chapter 58.

The stone must be drawn from the Sun <term>Sun: The alchemical name for gold.</term> and the Moon <term>Moon: The alchemical name for silver.</term>, and to have a true conjunction <term>Conjunction: The alchemical process of uniting two opposite substances.</term> and a true mixture, there must be from both of them the red man and the white woman <note>The "Red Man" (Gold/Sulphur) and "White Woman" (Silver/Mercury) are common alchemical personifications used to describe the union of active and passive principles.</note>; for the hot red man must operate upon the cold white woman, if one wishes for some fruit or perfect temperament to result and be formed. The same author, chapter 61.

The Philosophers <term>Philosophers: A term used by alchemists to refer to themselves and the masters of the "hermetic art."</term>, for the purpose of imitating nature in its generation, have taken the Sun and the Moon in order to draw from them their stone. They chose both after mature deliberation, because they feared that the Sun might escape <note>original: "s'en aliast" — likely meaning to vanish or be lost due to its high volatility or purity during the heating process.</note> because of its great perfection, purity, and subtlety when put to work; and seeing, on the other hand, that the Moon has many coarse and earthly parts, they nonetheless love each other like husband and wife. Because one is hot and the other cold, they could temper each other in their subtlety and earthiness, and because one contains the other, they have taken both in equal weight. The same author, page 109.

<note>A scholium is an explanatory note or commentary added to a text to clarify a specific point.</note>

Isaac <note>Likely Isaac Hollandus, a famous 15th-century Dutch alchemist whose writings were highly regarded for their clarity and practicality.</note> calls the black matter an "impure body" into which one must infuse whiteness. He states that this whiteness is the Moon, and then the stone—which is the whitened blackness—contains within itself the power and virtue that can never be separated from it. Anyone who is ignorant of this will never achieve anything good in this science, because by ignoring the roots, he will lack the judgment to administer the proper fire to join the spirit, the soul, and the body. To ensure that one does not imagine any chimeras <note>In this context, "chimeras" refers to unfounded fantasies, illusions, or false chemical theories.</note>, he says that the whole is made of the Sun, Moon, and mercury, which he calls "distilled common water"—that is to say, very well purified. Both [the Sun and Moon] are necessary for the reasons he provides, in equal weight. To achieve this, one must have patience because of the length of time required; but also, in this lengthiness, there is neither fear, nor dread, nor danger, as this path is the only one by which the Philosophers who have seen this truth have safely traveled.

### Text

<margin>Libavius <note>Andreas Libavius (c. 1555–1616) was a German physician and chemist known for his work "Alchemia," often considered one of the first systematic chemistry textbooks.</note></margin>
Mercury extracted from metallic bodies by means of mineral agents, and then employed for the dissolution of the Sun <term>Sun: The alchemical name for gold.</term> and the Moon <term>Moon: The alchemical name for silver.</term>, is useless and fruitless. Libavius, *On the Philosopher's Stone*, p. 67.

<margin>Lull <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher and polymath. Many alchemical works were attributed to him posthumously (Pseudo-Lull), focusing on the transmutation of metals.</note></margin>
Our stone is the conjunction of the Sun and the Moon, until the Sun has drawn the substance of the Moon into its own nature and its own color, which is accomplished with the fire of the stone. Lull, p. 71 of the *Codicil* and p. 132, beginning **with the dregs** <note>original Latin: "cum fecibes," likely a corruption of "cum faecibus."</note>.

Know that the perfect bodies improve the imperfect ones. The Sun is the father of all metals, and the Moon is the mother; even though the Moon receives its brightness from the Sun, upon these two depends the entire Magistery <term>Magistery: The mastery of the alchemical process, or the production of the Philosopher's Stone itself.</term>. Therefore, reduce them into living mercury, and not the vulgar <note>"Vulgar" refers to common, everyday materials (like standard mercury) as opposed to the "philosophical" or "living" versions prepared by the alchemist.</note> kind—that is to say, not volatile, but fixed. For the vulgar kind is volatile and full of phlegmatic coldness, which is why it needs to be reduced by fixed quicksilver <term>Quicksilver: Another term for mercury.</term>, which is hotter and drier in contrary qualities than vulgar quicksilver. Therefore I advise you, O my friends, that you work only with the Sun and the Moon, reducing them to their first matter, which is our sulfur and quicksilver: therefore, son, ser- <note>The catchword "ser-" likely anticipates a word such as "serve" or "service" on the following page.</note>

It explains the alchemical theory that "perfect" metals must be reduced to a "fixed" (non-volatile) state to act as the primary matter of the work.</summary>

Lull, whom everyone wishes to emulate, shows us clearly enough from how many pieces the stone is made; he says therefore that it is the conjunction of the Sun <term>Sun: The alchemical symbol for gold.</term> and the Moon <term>Moon: The alchemical symbol for silver.</term>, which is achieved with the fire of the stone, which is the Mercury, as we have already shown; this conjunction takes place in the blackness <note>The "blackness" (nigredo) refers to the first stage of the alchemical process, signifying decomposition or the "death" of the metals before their rebirth.</note>. But this stone is not finished unless the silver is entirely reddened to a very deep red color, and then this reddened and perfect matter improves and corrects the imperfect metals into gold, without it being necessary, however, to reduce anything into common and flowing mercury <note>Common or "vulgar" mercury is the liquid metal found in nature, which alchemists often distinguished from their "Philosophical Mercury" extracted from metals.</note> as many believe. Instead, it must be reduced into a black and impalpable matter proceeding from the two; and afterwards this powder, being black, dry, and subtle, dries, fixes, and refines the mercury which is added to it according to the order known to the sages. Thus, whoever seeks another path and another matter is gravely mistaken, and will finally throw themselves into an agonizing abyss.

In the same way that from one man another man is engendered, from a donkey another donkey, from a hen's egg a hen, and wheat comes from wheat, so from each like thing its like is made: from gold comes gold, and from silver, silver. The *Philosophers* <note>In alchemical texts, "Philosophers" refers specifically to practitioners of alchemy (the Hermetic Philosophy).</note> before beginning their work, having taken common gold and silver, must know if they are good and healthy or sick; for if they are sick, they must cure them with proper medicine, which is the proper preparation. <page-num>119</page-num>
# CHAPTER III.
->H iij<- <note>The signature mark "H iij" indicates the sequence of the printed pages in the original book's binding.</note>

### Scholium. <note>original: "Scholie." A scholium is an explanatory note or commentary intended to clarify a specific point in a text.</note>

Each thing begets its like; for if one extracts mercury from lead, that which is produced will result in lead. This extraction, therefore, will not be what we seek, nor is it the "egg" understood here, which is composed of good materials—namely, of purified and healthy gold and silver covered with crude mercury <note>Crude mercury refers to "virgin" mercury found naturally in its liquid state, rather than mercury chemically "revived" from ore by man.</note>—specifically that which comes directly from the mine, but very clean, which will serve as the skin [of the egg]. The whole is placed in a glass vessel, which will be the hard shell, beneath which one shall place a proper fire. 

O seekers and lovers of truth, I conjure you by the truth that I adore: flee and banish far from you all these extractors of mercury from lead. For although the Philosophers <note>In this context, "the Philosophers" refers to the established authorities of the alchemical tradition, such as Hermes Trismegistus or Geber.</note> say that one must extract mercury from Saturn <note>Saturn is the traditional alchemical name and symbol for the metal lead.</note>—which many call lead—I assure you, upon the oath I have sworn to truth itself, that it must not be understood as common lead, but as the "Lead of the Philosophers." Those who do not believe me will be deceived; and <margin>*Reasons for the scholium.*</margin> I protest that nothing makes me bring these writings to light but the charity and compassion I have upon seeing so many good people deceived by these rascally vagabonds. These men promise those who listen to them mountains of gold, and yet they have not the means to sustain or clothe themselves, which is easy to see by their actions.

<summary>The author warns that the mercury used in the Great Work must be natural "virgin" mercury rather than mercury extracted from common lead. He stresses that alchemical terms like "Saturn" are metaphorical and cautions readers against fraudulent alchemists who interpret these texts too literally.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Lead of the Philosophers, quicksilver, mercury, alchemical egg, fraud, gold and silver purification</keywords>

The Sun is the traditional alchemical symbol and name for gold.</note> with five ounces of antimony. The same [author], p. 35.

### Scholium.

Each generates its own kind; so that if one extracts mercury from lead, this offspring will produce lead. Therefore, this extraction is not what we seek, nor is the "egg" understood here in that way. This egg is composed of good materials—namely, of purified and very healthy gold and silver, covered with raw mercury <note>In alchemy, the "egg" refers to the sealed glass vessel and its contents, symbolizing the gestation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> that comes directly from the mine, yet is very clean, which will serve as the skin. All of this is placed in a glass vessel, which will be the shell, upon which a proper fire shall be applied. 

O seekers and lovers of truth, I conjure you by the truth that I adore: flee and banish from your company all those who extract mercury from lead. For although the Philosophers say that one must draw mercury from Saturn <note>original: "Saturne." In alchemical symbolism, Saturn represents the metal lead.</note>, which many call lead, I assure you—by the oath I have sworn to truth itself—that this must not be understood as common lead <note>The author is distinguishing between "vulgar" lead (the physical metal) and "philosophical" lead (a specific state of matter or a different substance altogether).</note>, but as the lead of the Philosophers. Whoever does not believe me will be deceived. I attest that nothing moves me to set down these writings <margin>*Reasons of the school.*</margin> except for the charity and compassion I feel seeing so many good people deceived by these hypocritical wanderers <note>original: "cafards de coureurs." This refers to "puffers" or fraudulent traveling alchemists who lived off the patronage of gullible victims.</note> who promise those who listen to them mountains of gold, and yet have not the means to support or clothe themselves—a fact easily seen by their actions.

<summary>The author clarifies that the "mercury" and "lead" used in the alchemical Great Work are not the common metals found in a smithy, but "philosophical" substances. He warns the reader against fraudulent alchemists who take these terms literally and bankrupt their followers.</summary>

### Scholium.

Though Alanus speaks of "transparent water," one must not, however, imagine a water through which one can see <note>The author is warning the reader not to expect a literal liquid like tap water.</note>, for this goes against the opinion of all the Philosophers, who desire only materials that symbolize <note>To "symbolize" here means to have a shared natural affinity or a "sympathy" of substance.</note> together; for what proportion would there be between wetting water <note>Common water that wets the hands.</note> and gold and silver? Moreover, no wetting water can dissolve gold and silver, seeing as the <term>philosophical dissolution <note>A process where a metal is reduced to its primary, liquid-like state without losing its "soul" or essential nature, unlike chemical dissolution in acids.</note></term> consists in reducing them to their principle, which is volatile, without them ever being able to be what they had been before, as we have stated previously. He therefore understands by this word "transparent"—meaning clear and shining—<term>common mercury <note>Quicksilver.</note></term> that has been very well purified, into which he intends for the gold and silver to be placed. And this water or mercury is what Hermes, Garlandius, and others name "wind." I am not ignorant of the power of strong water— <note>original: "l’eau for-", completed by the catchword "te" as "eau forte," or nitric acid.</note>

<summary>This page uses citations from Alchemical masters to argue that the "water" used in the Great Work is not common water, but a highly purified "philosophical" mercury referred to metaphorically as "wind."</summary>

...tion, and aqua regia <note>original: *eau regale*; a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids. The author is completing the word "dissolution" from the previous page.</note>, but I say that these waters gnaw and corrode gold and silver, which are afterwards returned to a mass as before, so that this corrosion—which is commonly and ignorantly called dissolution—is not at all that of which our Philosophers speak. I know also that the reduction of quicksilver <note>original: *argent vif*; mercury.</note> into a crystalline matter—clear, transparent, and solid—after having been as if dissolved by aqua fortis <note>original: *eau forte*; nitric acid.</note> made for the purpose (for not every aqua fortis does it) is suitable for making some masterpiece, but it is worth nothing in this work <note>The "Great Work" of alchemy, the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, which is the reason why I do not wish to give the instructions for it, by which someone might be misled.

->*Text.*<-

<margin>Egidius. <note>Refers to Aegidius de Vadis, a 16th-century alchemist known for his work *Dialogus inter Naturam et Filium Philosophiae*.</note></margin>
The principle of each thing results from its final intention; therefore, he who wants to make gold or silver will take the principle from them, as it is impossible to make gold and silver without gold and silver, because each thing produces its like. Egidius, page 3.

Join the soul <note>In alchemy, the "soul" often refers to the oily, inflammable, or coloring principle (sulfur).</note> with its body <note>The "body" refers to the solid, fixed metallic base.</note> by means of its spirit <note>The "spirit" refers to the volatile principle (mercury) that acts as a bridge between the soul and body.</note>, since the soul will by no means take its body except by means of its own spirit. The same, page 68.

There is nothing here but the brother and the sister—that is to say, the agent and the patient <note>The "agent" is the active, masculine principle; the "patient" is the passive, feminine principle that receives the action.</note>—and the sulfur and the mercury are engendered co-essential... <note>The text breaks off mid-word; "co-essential" suggests they share the same fundamental substance.</note>

<summary>The author clarifies that true alchemical dissolution is not the same as common acid corrosion and begins citing Egidius to explain that the "principle" of gold must come from gold itself, achieved through the union of soul, body, and spirit.</summary>

completely. The same, page 47.

*The ancients have said the work consists of two, and some have named these two joined together the "composite," inasmuch as these two make four, and in them there is dryness and humidity, spirit and vapor. No. 140 of the same.*

->Scholium.<-

Our author begins with the response that one is accustomed to making to a question such as this: "What do you wish to have in your garden?" Answer: "Cabbages, lettuce, and the like." Question: "And why then do you not take the seed of cabbages and lettuce?" Now, it is the case that the seed only proceeds from its plant, therefore it must be drawn from it. Likewise, he who wants to have gold, silver, iron, copper, brass, or lead, must draw from them their seed, which is commonly called <term>mercury</term> <note>In alchemical theory, Mercury is the "feminine" principle of fluidity and transformation, not merely the common liquid metal.</note> or <term>sulfur</term> <note>Sulfur represents the "masculine" principle of combustion, structure, and color.</note> by similarity of property. One must be so careful as not to mate a horse with a female donkey, or a male donkey with a mare to obtain a horse; for each species delights in its own species. Similarly, if one joins in this admirable composition a volatile, combustible matter with one that is incombustible and fixed <note>original: *fixe*; refers to a substance that remains stable and does not evaporate when exposed to high heat.</note>, one will have nothing but a bastard. This is the reason why our Author says that one must join the soul to its body, and not simply to "the" body, but to *its* body, to exclude the mis-

<summary>This page uses a biological analogy—planting seeds and breeding animals—to explain that the alchemical "seed" of a metal must be derived from the metal itself to ensure a "pure" result rather than a "bastard" or imperfect mixture.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "composition" and the union of soul and body, specifically addressing the Pythagorean concept of the soul's movement and the balancing of elemental qualities—heat, cold, dryness, and moisture—measured in "degrees" to achieve the alchemical Great Work.</meta>

<page-num>123</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER III.</header>

...metempsychosis <note>original: *teplychose* (likely a corruption of *metempsychose*); the Pythagorean doctrine of the transmigration of souls from one body to another.</note> of Pythagoras, who held that a soul, upon leaving the body in which it lived, should enter the first empty body it encountered. Our author adds that this happens by means of its spirit—otherwise nothing is accomplished—and yet, in all of this, there is only **form** and **matter**, **brother** and **sister**, **agent** and **patient**, which the ancient Philosophers have named the **composition**. This composition contains **dryness** in the third degree, **humidity** in the second, **coldness** in the first, and a **slackened heat** <note>original: *chaleur remise*; a gentle or moderated heat, rather than an intense, direct flame.</note>. For if this heat were at the beginning of the fourth degree, it would follow that it would burn everything, and we would have no need for external fire. But insofar as this heat is a slave, it must be set at liberty; he who does not understand this should study. Regardless, having disengaged this slow and slackened heat, we render it supreme; but dryness follows it, and these two, once united, bend the rebel <note>Likely referring to the volatile or "moist" element that resists stabilization.</note> to their will and put the prisoner to death. Consequently, the **crown** remains without controversy for this first slave <note>The "slave" (internal heat) becomes the "king" or "crowned" principle after being liberated and refined.</note>. 

But if one sets the heat one degree lower, dryness at the second degree, humidity at the first, and a slackened coldness, it will come to give us contentment. However, it will provide much more if the cold takes the upper hand of the two lower points, and humidity the two highest, with heat at the third degree, dryness at the second, and humidity at the first of the two points; this will be the **completed work**. But this shall be made clear when we speak of numbers and figures.

# CHAPTER III.

<margin>...firstly through gold and silver. The same [author], page 121.</margin>

->*Scholium* <note>A scholium is a scholarly commentary or explanatory note intended to clarify a text.</note><-

Many waste their time and deceive themselves by trying to extract the **tincture** <term>Tincture; in alchemy, the "soul" or coloring principle of a metal that has the power to transform other substances.</term>—that is to say, the yellow part of gold—hoping to later use this yellow to dye silver. But, by their own accounts, they do not dye any more silver than they have consumed <note>original: "eſteint", literally "extinguished" or "used up."</note> in gold. If this occurs as they describe, it is still done at a loss. Our Author states that gold is entirely essential in its nature; therefore, he does not wish to separate from it what many others believe should be removed. He silently teaches here that everything must be passed through the **chamois** <note>Alchemists filtered liquid mercury through chamois leather to remove physical impurities.</note>, and consequently, that silver can be passed through it as well. These are joined to form a single composition, which is the **mercury**—a point upon which all the **Philosophers** <note>The "Sages" or historical masters of alchemy.</note> agree, rather than seeking the secret in animals or vegetables. After he has indicated what must be taken from them, he concludes that the final operation is to join gold to the **red elixir** and silver to the **white elixir** <term>Red and White Elixirs; the two stages of the "Philosopher's Stone." The White Elixir transmutes base metals into silver, while the more potent Red Elixir transmutes them into gold.</term>, as we shall see clearly in the chapter on **fermentation** <note>The stage where the "Stone" is unified with a metal to act as a yeast, multiplying its power.</note>.

...[in]visible, because it departs invisibly, which is the reason why it is called the *fugitive slave* <note>original: "serf fugitif"; a common alchemical term for mercury, referring to its tendency to evaporate or "flee" when heated.</note>, and it cannot be burned. We have said previously that we had need of six mercuries, and have described four of them; at present, we shall speak of the other two, with a warning to the Reader to consider maturely what follows, before scoffing or criticizing. Many, reading this passage and others of a similar way of speaking—both those written before and those hereafter—which say that our mercury is **very clear water**; run after an infinity of waters that they call "dissolving" without knowing the definition of our **dissolution** <term>Dissolution; in alchemy, this refers to the process of reducing a solid metal back into its essential "seed" or liquid state, rather than simply melting it or dissolving it in acid.</term>, which (as was said above) is to reduce **gold** and **silver** into volatile matter by that very thing from which they took their principle <note>This refers to the alchemical axiom that a substance can only be truly dissolved by its own "mother" or the source of its metallic nature.</note>. This cannot be done except with a single [substance] that is not corrosive, as are all other [acidic] waters, regardless of the materials from which one knows how to make them. Therefore, **mercury** is a particular **virtue** <term>Virtue; a specific power or essential property hidden within a substance.</term> hidden within **common mercury** <note>original: "mercure vulgaire"; the ordinary quicksilver used in commerce, as opposed to the "Philosophical Mercury" sought by practitioners.</note>, and is known therein only by the wise Philosopher in this matter, just as the circle of the heavens is known to the Mathematician, and the surface line and the **indivisible point** <note>A geometric concept of a point that has position but no magnitude or parts.</note> to the Geometer; this virtue, thus considered, and not separated from the mercury...

common <note>original: "vulgaire"; refers to ordinary, store-bought substances rather than the specialized "philosophical" versions prepared by the alchemist.</note>, is named **clear water**, by which the seeds or **sulfurs** <term>Sulfur; in alchemy, this represents the active, combustible, and "masculine" principle of a metal, often associated with its color and soul.</term> that we wish to extract from **gold** and **silver** emerge more easily; this is therefore the **fifth mercury**; as for the **sixth**, it will be spoken of in its proper place. ### CHAPTER III.

<margin>*Warnings.*</margin>
virtue <note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion of the power or "virtue" of the substances.</note>; [rather] than in color: but what does our Author mean by these words, the **Sun**, the **Moon**, and **Azoth**? Are our stones dead? Certainly, just as we call a man and a woman "dead" if they produce no children, in the same way **gold**, **silver**, and **mercury** [are dead] while remaining as they are. But if they are joined and produce, then they shall be called "living"—that is to say, they will give proof of their power, seeing as there is nothing but living things that engender and produce. I would very much like those rare spirits who love this science to pay attention to what this Author says: namely, that **Azoth** <term>Azoth; a central concept in alchemy representing the universal solvent or the "Mercury of the Philosophers," often symbolized as the beginning and end of all things.</term> (which is not common mercury) is indivisible. This is because it either all disappears or all remains. For we call indivisible that which can be separated <note>The author uses a paradoxical definition here; he describes substances like wine as "divisible" because their components (spirit, salt, oil) are not "wine" once separated, whereas mercury always remains mercury.</note> into various parts of different natures, such as **wine**, which is divided into **brandy** <note>original: "eau de vie"; literally "water of life," referring to distilled spirit of wine.</note>, which is not wine in nature; into **salt**, which is not wine; and into **oil**, which is not wine—and yet all of these were within the wine. Similarly, from this **paper**, various parts can be drawn which will not be paper; and if these parts—even one of them—are separated, the wine or the paper will no longer be wine or paper. But with **mercury**, whatever one wishes to make of it, and whatever one mixes it with, it cannot be so ruined that, in the end, by industry <note>In this context, "industry" refers to the chemical skill and labor of the alchemist.</note> it does not return to being mercury; and from it, nothing will emerge that is not mercury, because it cannot be burned. I freely admit that it can be **precipitated** <term>Precipitate; to cause a substance to settle out of a solution as a solid, usually through a chemical reaction.</term>, but in its entirety, and not in part; it can be **congealed** <term>Congeal; in alchemy, to turn a liquid (like mercury) into a solid form.</term>, but in its entirety, and not in part. And if it does not burn, why then is it claimed that the blackness <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "oirceur" completes the word "noirceur" or "blackness," referring to the stage of putrefaction in the alchemical work.</note> arising by its means upon gold and

**A**ll the profit of the artist depends on **mercury**, the **Sun**, and the **Moon**; you shall reap what you have sown. *Desirable, p. 21.* <note>The Sun and Moon are standard alchemical metaphors for Gold and Silver, respectively.</note>

*There is a difference between the solar medicine and the lunar medicine, insofar as the solar contains solar sulfur, and the lunar contains lunar sulfur; for the lunar requires a very pure white sulfur, and the solar a very clean red sulfur. The same [author], p. 25.* <term>sulfur; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, masculine, and combustible principle of a substance, whereas mercury represents the passive and fluid principle.</term>

*The preparation of common mercury made with common **Sun** and common **Moon**—from the dissolution of these three without anything else whatsoever—the physical stone is made; and it cannot be made by any physical invention with other things. The same [author], p. 114.* <term>physical stone; another name for the Philosophers' Stone, the legendary substance capable of transmuting base metals into gold and silver.</term>

*Rebis is the first part of the work, the Elixir the second, the tincture is the third, and the medicine is the fourth: it is therefore quite clear that the Elixir is necessary for the **Azoth**, because in this operation the Elixir precedes the **Azoth**: for from the Elixir one draws the **Azoth**: now the* <term>Rebis; from the Latin "res bina" meaning "double thing," it refers to the hermaphroditic stage of the work where male and female principles are united.</term>

<vocab>mercury, Sun, Moon, sulfur, Rebis, Elixir, tincture, Azoth, physical stone</vocab>

### Scholium. <note>A "scholium" is an explanatory note or commentary on a text, common in scholarly and scientific works of this period.</note>

The **mercury** in which **gold** and **silver** have been dissolved is considered by some to be the **double mercury** of which **Trevisan** <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490) was a legendary Italian alchemist whose writings emphasized the use of mercury and gold.</note> speaks in his *Epistle to **Thomas of Bologna*** <note>Thomas of Bologna was the physician to King Charles V of France and a fellow alchemist.</note>. He uses the comparison of meat boiled in water: while he admits that the water was once common water, he denies that it remains so after the cooking. Similarly, the mercury that has caused the dissolution of gold and silver is no longer "common," even if the fat or the floating blackness <note>This refers to the "scum" or impurities that rise to the surface during alchemical heating.</note> has been removed. This mercury has acquired a certain power greater than it possessed before; for just as the **solvent** communicates its power to the thing dissolved, likewise the thing which is dissolved communicates its property to its solvent. Thus, from the two, a certain third thing is made, which is neither one nor the other, but something else entirely, holding the nature of both.

Now experience, which is the teacher of everyone, shows (as has already been said) that everything under the concavity of the heavens is **combustible** and **volatile**, with only **gold** and **silver** excepted. (Some add **glass** to this list, but they are ignorant of its combustion <note>Alchemists often debated whether glass was truly "fixed" or if it could be broken back down into its constituent parts by fire.</note>). If, therefore, the **artist** <note>In this context, the "artist" refers to the alchemist practicing the art.</note> works to obtain something **incombustible**, enduring, and **fixed**, why does he seek this fixed matter from that which is not fixed in itself? Will a person pay ten **crowns** <note>original: "eſcus"; a significant gold coin of the era.</note> to bail out someone whom they want to keep from being imprisoned for debt, when they themselves do not even have five **sous** <note>original: "ſols"; a coin of much smaller value.</note> to pay the person to whom they are indebted? The artist's entire gain,

<summary>This page explains the concept of "double mercury" as a mixture of mercury, gold, and silver that acquires new properties. It argues that the alchemist must start with "fixed" materials like gold and silver to achieve a "fixed" and incombustible result.</summary>

### Scholium. <note>A scholarly commentary or explanatory note added to a text to clarify a specific point.</note>

The mercury in which gold and silver have been dissolved is taken by some to be the "double mercury" of which Trevisan <note>Bernard of Trevisan (1406–1490), a famous alchemist often cited for his "Letter to Thomas of Bologna."</note> speaks in his *Letter to Thomas of Bologna*, through the comparison he gives of meat boiled in water. He freely admits that the water was once common water, but he denies that it remains so then <note>i.e., once it has become a broth.</note>; likewise, the mercury which has been the cause of the dissolution of gold and silver is no longer common, even though the grease or floating blackness <note>Refers to the "scum" or impurities that rise to the surface during the process.</note> has been removed from it. For this mercury has acquired some greater virtue <note>power or essential property</note> than it had before; for just as the solvent communicates its virtue to the thing dissolved, likewise the thing which is dissolved communicates its property to its solvent, so that from the two a certain thing is made which is neither the one nor the other, but something else entirely, possessing the nature of both. 

Now experience, which is the mistress of everyone, shows (as has already been said) that everything under the concavity of the heavens is combustible and volatile, with only gold and silver excepted (some add glass to this, but they are ignorant of its combustion). If therefore the artist <note>The alchemist.</note> labors to obtain something incombustible, everlasting, and fixed, why does he seek this fixed matter from that which is not so of itself? Will a person pay ten crowns <note>original: "escus" (écus), a gold or silver coin.</note> for someone whom one wants to imprison because they cannot pay, when they do not even have five sous <note>original: "sols," a coin of small value.</note> to pay the person to whom they are indebted? All the gain of the artist,

# CHEMICAL HARMONY

<margin>Aristotle.
<insert>100
203</insert></margin>
Take your most dear son <note>An alchemical metaphor for Gold.</note> and join him equally to his white sister <note>An alchemical metaphor for Silver.</note>; give him to drink the beverage of love <note>The mercurial solvent used to dissolve the metals.</note> until they are intoxicated and divided into very small parts. However, remember that all clean things suit clean things; otherwise, they will beget children who do not resemble them. Aristotle p. 163.

->Scholium<-

We have already seen the authority of Lully <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315), a Majorcan polymath whose name was frequently attached to later alchemical texts.</note> on a similar subject, but furthermore, in the treatise he titled *Apertorium* <note>Latin for "The Opener" or "The Unlocker."</note>, he begins thus: "Our sages affirm that there is but one stone composed of the four elements, having need of nothing whatsoever that is not of its nature." But this "one" must be understood rightly, and as we have already shown, the two seeds of the man and the woman, united and inseparable, though coming from two bodies, are not called "two." For we have already several times noted that gold and silver, once reduced to sulfur <note>The "sulfur of the philosophers," a state where the metals are broken down into their essential combustible principle.</note>, can no longer be separated or distinguished from one another. Therefore, being thus physically mixed, they are no more than one thing. 

Now, regarding what he says about some people mixing a metallic body into it, this is only done during fermentation <note>The final stage of the process where the "medicine" is stabilized by adding a small amount of pure gold or silver.</note>; this body is either silver for the "white" work <note>The stage aimed at creating silver.</note> or gold for the "red" work <note>The final stage aimed at creating the legendary gold-making stone.</note>. Aristotle afterwards marks for us the form of the composition, desiring that the gold and silver be equal—that is to say, as much of one as of the other as will be possible for us:

<summary>This page discusses the allegorical union of "the son" (gold) and "the white sister" (silver) to create a single substance, emphasizing that the Philosophers' Stone is a unified nature despite its dual components.</summary>

As for the matter of weight, having no balance <note>A weighing scale.</note>, we would be at a standstill; and furthermore, seeing as this is a matter approaching the generation of an animal, how shall we use weights, given that animals do not use them to conceive in their matings? I do not reject the balance, but I also say that judgment alone may suffice. But what is this "intoxication" <note>Referring to the "beverage of love" from the previous page, which dissolves the metals.</note> that reduces these bodies into very small parts? It is the mercury which is used to amalgamate <note>To blend or alloy metals with mercury.</note> them; when guided by the fire known to the wise Philosopher, it dissolves these bodies so thoroughly that it makes them float upon it in the form of a spider's web or a fine gauze. When they are drawn from there and placed into another vessel, they beget what is desired, and that for which they are destined. But above all, care must be taken that the gold, the silver, and the mercury are very clean and pure; otherwise, one would be working in vain.

This likely refers to the "Celestial Dog" or "Dog of Corascene," an alchemical symbol for a specific mercurial substance or a stage of purification.</note>, who will guard you from the beginning in your house, in this world and in the next. The same [author], p. 192.

The matter of the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>A term for alchemists, the "Philosophers of Nature."</note> is water, and this refers to the water of these three [components], and one needs neither more nor less. The **Sun** is the male, the **Moon** the female, and **mercury** the seed. But for the end that generation and conception may take place, the male must join the female, and besides this the seed is required; therefore, before <term>fermentation</term> <note>The stage where the "leaven" of gold or silver is added to the base matter to direct its transformation.</note>, the conception and impregnation must be performed. When the matter multiplies, it is said that the child grows in the mother’s womb; when it ferments, the soul is infused into the body, and the **Crowned King** grows. Dissolve the bodies and imbibe the spirit. One speaks of "bodies" in the plural because at least two are needed, and "spirit" in the singular because one suffices; and there is no seed without the matter of a body other than mercury. When one says "imbibe the spirit," this refers to the operation which fixes the mercury and multiplies the stone; to multiply is the same as to say to reiterate. The same [author], p. 204.

But do we have no need of any body other than gold? Listen to <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>: "His father is..."

...to speak of the first composition, that is, the **Sun** <note>Symbolizing gold or the masculine, hot, and dry principle.</note> and the **Moon** <note>Symbolizing silver or the feminine, cold, and moist principle.</note> is its mother; the father is hot and dry, begetting the <term>tincture</term> <note>The transformative essence or "dye" capable of transmuting metals.</note>, the mother is cold and moist, nourishing the offspring. If there were only one of these in our **Stone**, the <term>Medicine</term> <note>Another name for the Philosopher's Stone, viewed as a universal healer for both metals and the human body.</note> would never flow, nor would it dye; and if it did dye, it would dye but very little, and the **mercury** would vanish in smoke, insofar as there would be no receptacle for the tincture. The end of our secret is to possess a medicine which flows before the flight of the mercury; therefore, the <term>conjunction</term> <note>The chemical and symbolic union of opposites.</note> of these two is necessary in our work. The same [author], p. 227.

->*Scholium.*<- <note>A marginal or explanatory note; a commentary.</note>

It is madness, our charlatans say, to busy oneself with study, for one needs only a good recipe to make gold and silver. I agree in part, but who is he who will give it? **Geber** <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), the foundational figure of Arabic alchemy.</note>, **Arnaldus** <note>Arnaldus de Villa Nova (c. 1240–1311), a famous physician and alchemist.</note>, **Lully** <note>Raimundus Lullus (c. 1232–1315), whose name was attached to a vast body of influential alchemical writings.</note>, and all the other good authors exhort us to study. As has already been said, our present Author continues yet to admonish us charitably not to throw ourselves into this art until we know its principles, confirming tacitly in this passage what someone said: namely, that the **Physician** <note>The practitioner who applies knowledge to create a cure or a transmutation.</note> begins where the **Physicist** <note>A "Natural Philosopher" who studies the laws and substances of nature.</note> ends. This is to say that after one has the knowledge of the subject upon which one wishes to work, one can boldly...

<summary>This page explains that the "Medicine" requires both masculine (hot/dry) and feminine (cold/moist) principles to "fix" the mercury and prevent its evaporation. The author then argues against "charlatans" who rely on recipes, insisting that true alchemical practice must be preceded by a deep study of natural philosophy.</summary>

### CHAPTER III.

...continue: now, it is not said in this place simply <term>nature</term> <note>In alchemy, "nature" refers to the inherent power and process of growth and change within matter.</note>, but true nature; not that there are two natures, for one and the same nature works upon all subjects, accommodating itself according to the matter, but to remove all excuse from the lazy. For he who wishes to work on marble will use different tools than if he worked on wood, and if on gold, different ones than on <margin>clay</margin>clay; and therefore, he must recognize the hardness and softness of his material. Likewise, he who wishes to work in this art must know the matter of metals, their <term>dissolvent</term> <note>The "universal solvent" or "Alkahest" sought by alchemists to break substances down into their primary matter.</note>, and the difference between one and the other, and above all, to know well what it is he seeks—I say, that which he seeks. For out of a thousand, sometimes not even two are found who know it, contenting themselves with saying they want to make gold, which no man in the world <margin>No one can make gold.</margin> has ever done nor will ever be able to do, this work being reserved for God alone. 

Now, once knowledge is acquired of what our Author marks for us, the rest will be easy enough; for he will know that the **Dragon** <note>The "Dragon" is a frequent alchemical symbol for the volatile or raw starting material, often associated with mercury or "crude" ores that must be "tamed" or "killed" by fire.</note> dies by a small fire in wanting to kill his brother and his sister; that these three species are sufficient when joined; that they must be taken whole, pure, and clean; and that although one says "making gold," it is not making it, but only making it appear, by removing the <term>accidents</term> <note>In the philosophical sense, "accidents" are non-essential qualities (like color or tarnishing) that mask the true, perfect essence of the gold beneath.</note> that hide it from our sight. He will learn how the opinions of the true Chemical Philosophers agree on this matter, which is their **mercury**, where on the contrary all the [ancient] Philosophers contradict one another; for <margin>Thales.</margin>Thales <gloss>the <unclear>Milesian</unclear></gloss> says that simple water is the principle of the Universe, while others...

<summary>The author explains that alchemists do not "create" gold, but rather reveal its true nature by removing impurities, emphasizing that only God can create matter while the chemist merely facilitates natural processes.</summary>

Take your matter, mix it in a round glass vessel in a reverberatory furnace <note>A furnace that reflects heat back onto the material rather than applying direct flame.</note> for four days; <margin>4 days</margin> for the first two days the fire shall be slow, on the third day strong, and on the fourth even stronger for twenty-four hours. Let it cool, open your vessel, and you will find your matter in a mass. Grind it finely and put it in a vessel to dissolve and congeal <note>A cycle of liquefying and solidifying a substance to purify it.</note> over hot ashes <note>A method of providing gentle, consistent heat.</note> without further grinding; simply dissolve and congeal it in the same vessel. Do this twenty-four <margin>24 times</margin> times: then take one part of it and cast it upon very pure gold, and it will become a very red powder. Take one part of this powder and put it over a hundred parts of very clean quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, and leave the glass vial in the hot ashes for twenty-four hours, and it will <margin>24 h.</margin> become oil. Cast one part of this over a hundred parts of refined Moon <note>The alchemical term for silver.</note>, and it will become the very finest Sun <note>The alchemical term for gold.</note>. *The same author.*

->*Scholium*<-
<note>original: "Scholie"; a marginal note or explanatory commentary on a text.</note>

The Sage says, listen to everything, but also test everything; if all those who seek this admirable <term>work<gloss>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus), the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</gloss></term> had well learned and practiced this lesson, the multitude of peddlers, charlatans, forgers, and sellers of recipes would not be so great, because finding no one who would listen to them, they would be constrained to hang and strangle themselves like Judas, or to learn some trade to earn their living. Certainly, it is a deplorable thing in this age that the hunger for gold is so great that it gives no relief, even to the greatest of men in their amassing of it, not considering that death is at their heels; and notwithstanding, they believe the first deceiver who promises to make them gold. Test, says the Sage, all spirits; test, say the Philosophers, the matter that is presented to you for <term>dyeing<gloss>The alchemical process of changing the "color" or nature of a metal, essentially transmuting it.</gloss></term> metals into gold or silver. Our <term>tincture<gloss>The potent alchemical substance used to perfect base metals; another name for the Elixir or Stone.</gloss></term>, they say, is fixed, similar to that which nature gives in the mine, and it endures all the forces and tests of fire, which the work of the <term>blowers<gloss>A derisive term for "puffers" or false alchemists who focused solely on the physical heat of the furnace rather than true philosophical principles.</gloss></term> will not do, as has been said; thus they ordinarily seek hiding places to sell their false—

hour, indeed in a moment. *Roger Bacon, ch. 7 of his Mirror.* <note>Reference to Roger Bacon’s *Speculum Alchemiae* (The Mirror of Alchemy), a highly influential 13th-century text.</note>

### Scholium.

It is commonly said that "common error makes the law," <note>original: *l'erreur commun faict la loy*; a reference to the legal maxim *communis error facit jus*, suggesting that a widely held belief becomes an accepted rule.</note> but I say that the error of the **ignorant** does not dictate the law to the **learned**. The ignorant insist that the **medicine of the Philosophers** <gloss>The Philosopher's Stone or Elixir.</gloss> purifies all **metals**, and as they say, reduces them to **gold**, if the medicine is red, or to **silver**, if it is white. This cannot be—I mean regarding their common medicine and first preparation. For no **natural agent**, acting according to nature, acts beyond its own degree, unless it acts upon a subject that possesses some quality similar to itself, which makes it susceptible to such action. Through this property of the subject and its capacity for being acted upon, the agent imprints and imparts to it all that it can. 

**For example**: a candle lit in a room will illuminate the surrounding air, but the air will not receive more light than is present in the flame of the candle; otherwise, the flame would be acting beyond its degree. However, if one brings a **crystal** near this flame, one will see within it a greater light than that which is in the flame of the candle itself. This happens because of that property or **susceptibility** that the **crystal** has for receiving this light, and not otherwise. The same is true of our **medicine**, from which all imperfect or "dirty" metals cannot receive the pu... <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely intended to be "purity" (pureté) or "power" (puissance).</note>

<vocab>medicine of the Philosophers, metals, gold, silver, natural agent, crystal, flame, susceptibility</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that the Philosopher's Stone does not work indiscriminately on all materials, but requires a "susceptible" subject, using the analogy of a candle flame's interaction with air versus a crystal.</summary>
<keywords>Roger Bacon, Scholium, natural agents, susceptibility, transmutation theory, light analogy, purification of metals</keywords>

# CHAPTER XV.

<margin>
fine
</margin>

...purity that one proposes to achieve, if one ignores the order and manner of the preparation of both the **agent** <gloss>the active substance, like the Elixir</gloss> and the **patient** <gloss>the substance being acted upon, like the base metal</gloss> before the **projection**. <term>projection: the final stage of the Great Work where the "medicine" is cast upon molten metal to transmute it into gold or silver.</term> Above, the preparation of the **medicine** <note>In alchemy, "medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the Elixir of Life.</note> was written quite extensively for those with a capable understanding; for this reason, we shall say only that the **red medicine** must be cast upon fine silver, which requires only **fixation** <term>fixation: the process of making a volatile substance stable and resistant to the heat of fire</term> and **tincture**, <term>tincture: the coloring property of the Stone that penetrates and transforms the essence of the metal</term> and the **white medicine** goes upon tin, which requires only fixation. For the others, it is possible, but with quite great difficulty.

<margin>
The gold that is eaten <note>original: "L'or qui se mange"; a reference to *Aurum Potabile* or potable gold, a medicinal liquid gold believed to grant longevity.</note>
</margin>

Note that everything that is transmuted into another is no longer what it was before, and through this loss of what it once possessed, it corrupts entirely—experiencing every kind of corruption—in order to become nourishment for another. As Solon remarked at the *Banquet of the Seven Sages* in Plutarch's *Opuscula*: "Every transmutation follows the nature of the transmuting agent, and the transmuting agent does not take on the nature of the thing transmuted." Therefore, if the transmuting agent is volatile and combustible, that which is transmuted will be the same. 

**Pico della Mirandola** <note>Italian Renaissance philosopher (1463–1494) who wrote on the Kabbalah and alchemy.</note> and others note having seen the **projection** performed in several ways; this does not, however, indicate different medicines, but a single one that can be mixed with various materials—such as wax, soap, tallow, **vitriol**, <note>A general term for sulfate minerals, often used as catalysts or solvents in alchemical recipes.</note> and the like—which vanish in the fire. The single medicine then attaches and unites with the molten **metal**, from which it separates the impurity and perfects the rest. By this differing manner of projection, even those most learned in other sciences are deceived by the belief they adopt that there are diversities of ways and means...

<summary>This page explains the final "projection" stage of alchemy, distinguishing between the red and white medicines and their effects on silver and tin. It also cites classical and Renaissance authorities like Plutarch and Pico della Mirandola to argue that while projection methods vary, the underlying transformative medicine remains one and the same.</summary>

<keywords>projection, red medicine, white medicine, fixation, tincture, transmutation, Solon, Plutarch, Pico della Mirandola, vitriol, metal, Aurum Potabile</keywords>

...and orders for purifying and perfecting imperfect and dirty metals. Now, the purity and impurity of metals is known by their weight, insofar as the heaviest is the most excellent; as proof, if one draws all the metals separately through the same hole of a draw-plate <term>draw-plate: a metal plate with graduated holes used to pull metal into wires of consistent thickness</term> and cuts them to the same length, one will find that if one weighs a dram <term>dram: an old unit of weight, here defined as 72 grains</term> <margin>72 grains</margin> [of gold, the heaviest metal], silver will only weigh thirty-six grains, and lead the same, copper thirty, iron twenty-six, steel twenty-seven, and tin twenty-five. Thus, the cause [of metal density] comes from the perfect mixing of the components, and from the purity or impurity of these, as well as the exclusion of air, perfect cooking <gloss>the alchemical process of "digestion" or heating</gloss>, and the evaporation of moisture, as observed by Libavius <note>Andreas Libavius (c. 1555–1616), a German physician and alchemist famous for writing the first systematic chemistry textbook, *Alchymia*.</note> on page 495 in his *Difference of Alchemy* <note>original: "difference de l'Alchymie"; this refers to a specific discussion in Libavius's work regarding the distinguishing characteristics of substances.</note>.

The sulfur of the Philosophers is a living fire, simple, vivifying other dead bodies and ripening them; it supplies what they lack by nature, seeing as there is more ripeness <note>original: "meureté," referring to the alchemical state of being fully 'digested' or perfected by heat</note> in it than it needs itself. # CHAPTER XVI.

Ee iij <note>This is a signature mark used by early printers to help bookbinders assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

[...itself, the earth is the] receptacle of the germ, and the water is the matrix of the seed. *Treatise on Sulfur, page 6.* <note>original: "Traicté du soulphre." This refers to the work of Michael Sendivogius, a famous 17th-century alchemist.</note>

<margin>Aristotle, Book 8 <note>Historical citation error: Aristotle's *On the Generation of Animals* consists of only five books; the author may be citing a later commentary or a different collection.</note> of *On the Generation of Animals*, chapter 8, distinguishes the offspring <note>original: "geniture"</note> or germ from the seed.</margin>
Fire acting against air produces sulfur; air acting against water produces salt; water acting against earth produces mercury; but the earth, finding no other element against which it might act, can produce nothing itself; instead, it retains in its center what the other three have produced, so that there are only three principles <term>three principles: known as the "Tria Prima" (Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt), these were considered by Paracelsian alchemists to be the fundamental components of all matter</term> for which the earth remains both matrix and nurse. *The same [author], page 40.*

The body is the earth, the spirit is the water, and the soul is the fire (or the sulfur of gold). The spirit increases only the quantity of the body, but the soul—or the sulfur, or the fire—increases its virtue <term>virtue: the inherent medicinal or transformative power of a substance</term>. But since there is more spirit (that is, water) by weight than fire, the spirit exalts itself and oppresses the fire, drawing it to itself, so that each of these two increases in virtue, and the earth—which is the medium or middle ground between them—grows in weight. *The same [author], page 43.*

<summary>This page explains the elemental origin of the three alchemical principles—Sulfur, Salt, and Mercury—positing the Earth as a passive "nurse" that contains the products of the other three elements. It further aligns these principles with the tripartite division of Body (Earth), Spirit (Water), and Soul (Fire/Sulfur).</summary>

Physicians and painters are indebted to the researchers of this science [Alchemy], who, because they did not understand or know the Mercury and Sulfur of the Philosophers, mixed common quicksilver with common sulfur, and with industrious effort created what is called **cinnabar** <note>A bright red mineral (mercury sulfide) used as a pigment. The author distinguishes between the common pigment and the alchemical "cinnabar."</note>. The Philosophers have used this same name for their matter when it has risen into **small trees** <note>This refers to the "Philosophical Tree," a branching, crystalline structure that forms during alchemical sublimation</note> and turns red there. Others, hearing and reading that we draw our Mercury from Saturn, amuse and deceive themselves—as we said before—by trying to extract mercury from lead <term>Saturn: the alchemical name for lead, associated with the heaviness and "darkness" of the metal.</term>. 

Now, the mercury which we use to perfect the imperfect [metals] is not made before one's eyes [found in nature], but must instead be extracted from **perfect bodies** <term>perfect bodies: gold and silver, which alchemists believed reached a state of metallic perfection that other metals had not yet achieved</term>. This mercury carries its own sulfur and cannot exist without it; indeed, it is often called sulfur itself. This sulfur is the **fiery virtue** <term>virtue: the inherent power or active property of a substance</term> hidden within, which makes this mercury dry and powdered. When this powder is moistened by its own water, it is turned into something like a white foam floating upon the water. However, it must be noted that Mercury is understood in two ways (or for two things) by the Philosophers: namely, as a **volatile matter** in fire, and as a **fixed matter** that remains within it. If the diligent student carefully considers the passages of good authors, they will easily recognize the reason for the variety of so many names. For now, let us be content to learn...

# CHAPTER XVII.

<meta>Signature: Ee iiij</meta>

that common **quicksilver** <term>quicksilver: common liquid mercury, which alchemists distinguished from the "mercury of the philosophers" needed for their work</term> is not at all that of the Philosophers, any more than common sulfur is. We readily admit that ours cannot appear except through the help of the common sort; however, we also say that the common sort abandons ours as soon as it has emerged from its two bodies, with which it must afterwards be joined. But whether this is achieved with skill, God and those who have performed it know. As for common sulfur, it has no access nor entry into our matter; ours being only the **hot, dry, and drying virtue** <note>In the theory of the four humors/elements, sulfur was associated with heat and dryness, acting as the "masculine" or active principle that "fixes" the mercury.</note>, which emerges equally from the components along with our mercury—of which we have previously discussed at great length. 

At present, it remains for us to know whether we must pay heed to the **stars and seasons** <note>Many alchemists believed that the "Great Work" should begin in the Spring (under the sign of Aries) or that specific planetary alignments were necessary for success.</note> before beginning this work, and whether it is easy to untie and understand all the **riddles** <note>Alchemical literature is famous for its "Enigmas"—coded language and allegorical images designed to protect secrets from the "unworthy."</note> established on this subject.

# CHAPTER XVII.
<meta>Ink bleed-through from the reverse page is visible in the right margin.</meta>

...various chapters, and we have done it this way because otherwise it would have been known as well by the wicked as by the good; and we have found this science by ourselves, which is most true and most certain. *Geber, Book 3, ch. 91.* <note>Geber refers to the legendary 8th-century figure Jabir ibn Hayyan; the text emphasizes that alchemical secrets are intentionally scattered across different chapters to hide them from the "unworthy."</note>

<margin>Bonus. <note>Petrus Bonus of Ferrara, a 14th-century physician and alchemist.</note></margin>
Alchemy does not use demonstrations, because it neither proves nor is proven as other things are, nor does it express what it contains like the others, insofar as it strives to speak obscurely, it being nearly impossible to show this art through logical reasoning. *Bonus, in "The New Pearl," p. 18.* <note>original: "Marguerite nouuelle," referring to the *Pretiosa Margarita Novella*, a famous alchemical treatise written around 1330.</note>

Avicenna <note>The Latinized name of Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), whose works on medicine and philosophy were central to medieval learning, though he was often skeptical of the possibility of metallic transmutation.</note> nor any other <term>natural philosopher: a scholar who studies the laws of the physical world, the precursor to the modern scientist.</term> has ever been able to confirm the principles of Alchemy through natural reasons; for that which is **fixed** destroys the specific form—that is to say, that which is **fixed** destroys the specific form of the **volatile**, preventing it from fleeing. *The same [Bonus], p. 20.* <note>In alchemy, "fixing the volatile" refers to the process of making a substance stable under heat so that it no longer evaporates or "flees" the fire.</note>

</margin>
If it is true, the curious but not subtle seeker will say, that one must pay no regard either to the stars, nor to the time, nor to the hours, then why is it that the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" refers specifically to the "Adepts" or masters of alchemy.</note> tell us to begin working in the month of March? Why should we wait for the conjunction of **Mercury** with **Saturn**, why that of the said **Mercury** with **Mars**, then with **Jupiter**, then with the **Moon**, then with the **Sun**, without which nothing will be accomplished? Certainly, just as the season in the month of March is temperate, and the air there is neither too hot, nor too cold, nor too dry, nor too humid, so too is it desired that at the beginning, the heat by which the <term>cooking: the process of heating the matter in a sealed vessel over a long period.</term>, putrefaction, or corrosion of our <term>chaos: the "Materia Prima" or raw, unrefined substance the alchemist begins with.</term> or mixture must be performed, should be gentle. This is so that through this gentleness, the whole may embrace and unite itself better and more easily; for if the heat were too great, the bond, glue, paste, and gum which must unite the male and the female <note>The "male" and "female" represent the active (sulfur) and passive (mercury) principles of matter which must be joined.</note> would vanish, and the two bodies would remain dry without any production, neither of blackness, nor of branchings, <note>referring to "dendritic" growth, where crystals form patterns resembling tree branches inside the glass vessel.</note> nor of any other color. Indeed, it is a true thing that our **sea water**—whose heart cannot support an overly violent heat without falling into a fever—would die, and by its death, our entire work would be ruined. Let the heat of the season in this month, therefore, serve as a model for the conduct of our work, and thus it will serve—

<summary>This page explains that the alchemical instruction to begin work in March is not about astrology, but about mimicking the temperate, gentle heat of Spring to prevent the "chaos" from drying out before it can bond. It emphasizes the need for a gentle "cooking" to ensure the union of the substance's internal male and female principles.</summary>
<keywords>March, temperate heat, chaos, mercury, conjunction, male and female, sea water, putrefaction</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 446, completing a sentence regarding the symbolic relationship between the seasons and the alchemical process, before moving into the planetary correspondences of the Great Work.</meta>

# CHAPTER XVII.

...[we] will speak of the conduct and likeness of the other months and seasons of the year. When it comes to the conjunctions of the planets, that of **Mercury** with **Saturn** is when blackness appears on the matter due to the <term>melancholic humor: one of the four bodily humors, associated here with the "Nigredo" or black stage of alchemy.</term>. That with **Mars** is the gray color—though others say it is the reddish-yellow color—due to the yellow <term>choler: or bile, representing a stage of "cooking" or heat.</term> which is slightly cooked. That of **Jupiter** is the first whiteness. That of the **Moon** is the great whiteness, due to the <term>phlegm: the cool, moist humor associated with the "Albedo" or white stage.</term>. And that of the **Sun** is due to the great redness and the fire or blood. That of **Venus** occurs when love or the heating takes place between the two substances.

It is in this fashion that we must understand the sayings of the **Philosophers**, both ancient and modern. This truth means that we do not need to expand further upon the discourse of these celestial sciences, since they are not necessary to us in this work, any more than is an understanding of all the enigmas. To untie those riddles, one would sooner have to bring the authors who proposed them back to life than to try to explain and interpret them. 

For who is the geometer who can <term>square the circle: a famous mathematical problem of constructing a square with the same area as a circle, used here to represent the "impossible" task of alchemical transformation.</term>, reduce this square into a triangle, this triangle into a line, and this line into a point (which is indivisible), which contains as much within itself as it did while it was a line, a triangle, a quadrangle, or a round circle? Would one not say that this is entirely impossible? And yet the chemical **Philosopher** performs this through the accomplishment of this art, and without this, he understands nothing of it; a man of the...

# CHAPTER XVII.

...thanks to the greatest silence they can maintain, being so joyful that they see the content is greater than the <margin><insert>Joyous marriage</insert></margin> container, laughing within themselves to see that the ignorant mock them, believing it impossible for the content to be greater than the container, and that one part should contain fifty in weight, number, quantity, and quality. <note>This alchemical paradox refers to the "Multiplication" of the Stone, where a tiny amount of the medicine—the content—has the power to transmute a vastly larger volume of base metal—the container.</note> Let us conclude this treatise, then, with <margin><unclear>... ... ...</unclear></margin> some comparisons and dreams that have been communicated to us by one of our friends skilled in this <term>science: here meaning Alchemy, the "Sacred Science" of transmuting matter and spirit.</term>, and by this notable sentence: that truth is but one. This is witnessed by the consensus of all the **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophes"; in this context, it specifically refers to "Alchemists" or "Philosophers by Fire."</note> cited in this treatise, who assure us that to acquire the desired perfection in this art, there is only a single **matter** drawn from two by one, within a single **vessel** in form—though in some others because of greater capacity, the matter grows by addition—a single **fire**, and a single **operation**. Let us not find it strange if authors have discoursed so diversely and obscurely of this science, seeing that it is common for men to despise and value as nothing the most excellent things in the world when they seem easy to them, and to praise magnificently and hold in admiration that which can only be acquired with much pain, labor, and sweat. He who prevents himself from falling into this vice along with the common people is not a man of little judgment.
