<header>CHEMICAL HARMONY</header>

<margin><unclear>Comprom</unclear> <unclear>ealy</unclear></margin>
...is the quantity and quality of lead, in the search for which man either profits or loses; likewise of gold through the **Sun**, and silver through the **Moon**. It is this which moved the **Alchemists** to name lead **Saturn**, gold the **Sun**, and silver the **Moon**. However, they differ from the **Astrologers**, who assign copper to **Jupiter**, while the Alchemists give him tin; to **Venus**, the Astrologers give tin, whereas the Alchemists give her copper. <note>This reversal of planetary associations for tin and copper was a common point of divergence between celestial astrology and terrestrial alchemy in the 17th century.</note> Both, however, assign iron and sulfur to **Mars**, and quicksilver to **Mercury**. 

If the knowledge of what is commonly called the **Philosophers' Stone** <note>original: "pierre des Philosophes"</note> could be acquired through debate, it would be somewhat easy to obtain; but those who have grasped it by the right end have only cared to write of it to prove that it is real, and to provide the means for curious seekers to gain that same knowledge. They describe it through riddles, similes, or examples. 

Many others, however, acting out of ostentation after having consumed their own means and those of others in fantasies—furnaces, vessels, fires, and mineral, animal, and vegetable drugs—amuse themselves without understanding the true meaning of the authors. They attempt to draw their principle from volatile and combustible materials, not believing that "each produces its like," <note>A fundamental alchemical maxim: *ours produces ours*. Just as a tree produces a tree, the "seed" of gold must come from a metallic nature, not from unrelated substances.</note> which is clearly seen in the writer of the *Chemical Collection* <note>original: "Fasciculus Chymicus"</note> printed in Paris at the house of Nicolas de la Vigne in 1631 under the name of **Arthur Dee**, chief physician to the Emperor of Russia. The arguments he presents in each chapter are so poorly joined, and his conclusions so ill-fitting and poorly drawn, that it is impossible for them to be worse. But seeing that his principle...

### CHAPTER XVII,

...principle <note>Continuing from the previous page: "Just as a tree whose principle..."</note> is drawn from a combustible thing, its product cannot be stable. In the **expurgatory index** <note>original: "indice expurgatoire"; a curated list intended to separate reliable texts from fraudulent or "cluttered" ones.</note> which will soon see the light of day, God willing, the good authors will be distinguished from the scribblers. I speak of those of whom we have had knowledge, for it is very difficult to have seen or known all the books that have treated this subject until now; nor should one believe that all books bearing the name of an author were actually composed by him. 

Now, I maintain that no one can understand fundamentally, nor distinguish a good author from a bad one, if he has not seen and does not know very well how to **extract his fire** without destroying the matter, and how to **nourish it**. For if these two points are unknown, all the rest is as well. The understanding of these two points may have been easier for certain other more refined spirits than for **David Laigneau**, who spent twenty-two years in this research before becoming Physician to the King, and who composed and arranged his **Harmony** <note>Referring to Laigneau’s work, *Harmonie Chimique* (1636), which attempted to reconcile the conflicting statements of various alchemists.</note> not upon the model of this aforementioned "thorny and brushy bundle" <note>original: "fagot brossailleux, & espineux"; a disparaging metaphor for a poorly constructed or confusing book.</note>—which, along with its author (whom I do not believe to be the one whose name it bears), deserves nothing but the fire. 

Let us not, therefore, be carried away by the **puffers** <term>souffleurs: a derogatory term for amateur or fraudulent alchemists who focused on the physical bellows (blowers) rather than the philosophical theory.</term> and deceivers; let us hold fast to the truth, for which God will show us the way and grant us possession—not only of this treasure <note>The Philosophers' Stone.</note>, if He deems it necessary for us, but of the heavenly one through the intercession of our Lord **Jesus Christ**, our only Savior and Redeemer, to whom with the Father and the Holy Spirit be praise, honor, and blessing forever and ever. **Amen**.

<summary>David Laigneau reflects on his twenty-two years of alchemical study, warning readers against fraudulent texts and "puffers" while emphasizing that true chemical understanding requires mastery of "fire." The text concludes with a prayer, situating the search for the Philosophers' Stone within a framework of Christian devotion.</summary>

<keywords>David Laigneau, Harmonie Chimique, puffers, expurgatory index, alchemical fire, Physician to the King, spiritual alchemy</keywords>

# THE PRINTER TO THE Reader.

Having recovered some papers already aged by time, and examining them with several of my friends who are expert in this **mystical science** <note>original: "science mystique"; referring here to alchemy and the hermetic arts.</note>, they found it appropriate to offer to the **studious seeker** <note>original: "curieux studieux"; a common 17th-century term for a dedicated student of natural philosophy and the hidden arts.</note> the following pieces, chosen from among them. As these are in no way contrary to the preceding treatise, enjoy them, therefore, to your heart's content.

<summary>In this brief preface, the printer informs the reader that he has discovered additional ancient alchemical manuscripts and, after consulting experts, has decided to include them as a relevant appendix to the main book.</summary>

The moisture they had carried away from the said sea, and that which had been added to them, surpassed them by three parts; but seeing themselves in such a state, and fearing that this humidity, by cooling them too much, might prevent them from engendering <note>In alchemical texts, "engendering" refers to the creation of the Philosopher’s Stone, often described as a biological birth resulting from the union of metallic principles.</note>, they arranged to have a suitable fire under their bed to gradually dry out or separate this added moisture. <margin>The Action <note>This refers to the practical laboratory "work" or experiment being performed.</note>.</margin>

### CHAPTER XVII.

<margin>1st Action. Ceration.</margin>
Entrance <note>original: "l'entree"; in alchemy, this refers to the ability of the Stone to penetrate or "sink into" the pores of base metals during transmutation.</note> was lacking to him; to remedy this, he was advised to place himself in the **stove** <term>stove (estuve): a controlled heating apparatus, like a vapor or sand bath, used here to soften the substance.</term>, wherein, being situated, they poured **sea water** <note>original: "l'eau de la mer"; likely a symbolic term for the mercurial liquid or "Philosophical Mercury" used to moisten the dry matter.</note> upon him drop by drop, until, having drunk a sufficient quantity, he himself melted upon the fire. This astonished his attendants, who promptly removed him from the said fire; and having placed him in the cold air, he regained his face and his strength. However, his body was separated into various and very minute parts, each as strong as the others. But what was truly admirable was his great courtesy and kindness; for, entering into combat with any of his inferiors—for there has been but one **Hercules** <note>Hercules: Frequently used in alchemy to represent the "fixed" and invincible power of the Stone which can overcome the "monsters" of metallic impurity.</note> in the world—after having dispossessed them of all their heritages, Empires, Kingdoms, and Principalities, he gave them more than he had taken from them.

<margin>Projection.</margin>
He rendered them greater lords than they had been before, and their duration was, is, and shall be until the consummation of the ages. To God be the glory, the honor, the praise, and the blessing forever and ever <note>original: "au siecle des siecles"; literally "from age to ages," a traditional liturgical closing.</note>, through His only Son our Lord and **Redeemer**, who lives and reigns with Him and His **Holy Spirit**.

<summary>The text describes the final preparation of the Philosopher's Stone through ceration (softening) and its subsequent power of "projection," whereby it transmutes base metals into something greater than they were. The narrative concludes with a traditional religious doxology, attributing the success of the work to Divine power.</summary>

forever and ever. Amen

The entire business in this admirable search is comprised in these few words.

<margin><image-desc>A small, dark ink sketch or symbol resembling a figure or a stylized chemical apparatus with a long vertical line extending downwards.</image-desc></margin>
Extract the **black sulfur** <note>original: "soulphre noir"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the active, combustible principle, and "black sulfur" refers to the substance in its initial, unrefined state of the Nigredo or "blackness."</note>, stinking and bitter, in the form of an impalpable powder, from our **chaos** <term>chaos: the "materia prima" or raw, disorganized starting material of the Great Work.</term> or confused mass; which sulfur, when thrown into sufficient water, blackens it; and if one rubs a plate of **copper** with it, the copper whitens; and if the said plate is plunged therein while red-hot from the fire, the whiteness will penetrate further, though not to the center.

<margin>Good</margin>
Turn this powder into a paste with its own water, etc.

Reduce this paste into a powder.

<margin>Repeat 9 <note>original: "R 9"; likely an instruction to repeat the process nine times, or a reference to a specific recipe number.</note></margin>
Repeat this reduction into paste, and this paste into powder, until the **whiteness** <note>The "Albedo" stage, signaling the purification of the matter.</note> appears, and then the **yellow** <note>The "Citrinitas" stage.</note>, and then the **red** <note>The "Rubedo," the final stage of the Great Work.</note>.

Give the **soul** <term>soul (ame): the volatile principle or "life force" that must be reunited with the "body" of the matter to complete the Stone.</term> to this dead matter, and being **revivified**, it will satisfy you. For then you will find that it is **mineral**, since it is drawn from minerals; **vegetable**, since it increases itself; and **animal**, once it has received the soul, without which it is fruitless. If you have failed, correct your fault in the same matter, since more easily...

assembly who were content to admire them without going any further, except to cause them trouble. <margin>Union of two within the third.</margin>
Amidst all the contentment of these three, suddenly Venus <note>Symbolizing the force of attraction or "chemical love" that draws substances together</note> goads the rooster to unite with the hen; and having caressed her, they seek (unlike the others) a secluded and covered place to enjoy their loves. They see the great and spacious maw of the serpent, into which they enter without difficulty; but alas! they did not know what was to happen to them.

and</margin>
This serpent, whose nature is cold (at least to the touch), had beneath it a small fire that warmed them, and caused the venom <note>original: "venin"; in alchemy, "venom" or "poison" often refers to the transformative solvent or the potent, unrefined state of the Philosophical Mercury</note> it possessed to penetrate more easily. As soon as the two birds had entered into it, it snapped its maw shut and trapped them entirely.

### RESPONSE TO A
### question.

Regarding the question proposed concerning the <term>mercury of the philosophers</term> <note>A term for the "living" or "spiritualized" essence of mercury, which alchemists distinguished from "vulgar" or common quicksilver</note> which adheres to metals—namely, whether it should be understood as common mercury? I say that this must be demonstrated more clearly than has been done by anyone I have seen until now, using an example familiar and known to everyone. To nourish and recover a person from an illness, one who can neither eat nor swallow anything solid, what will be done to increase their strength and add new flesh to their flesh? Is it not customary to place mutton, veal, poultry, herbs, and other things into common water, so that by boiling, their virtue and internal <term>balm</term> <note>In Paracelsian medicine, the "balm" or "balsam" is the life-sustaining liquid or "radical moisture" that preserves the body from decay</note> might be joined...

<summary>This page describes the transition of an alchemical substance from a dry powder to a buttery "amalgam" and begins an explanation distinguishing the "mercury of the philosophers" from common mercury using a medical analogy of restorative broth.</summary>

476

...[inte]rior of these matters and implant them into this water as before; now, being thus impregnated, is it still common water? In the beginning, it was without nourishment, without smell or flavor; now it has nourishment and flavor, not of itself, but within it, which it has drawn from others. And of that which it has drawn, it—which is a medium and an intermediary—carries and communicates it to the parts which provide proper nourishment, and which are suitable to them. Likewise, there is common mercury <note>original: "☿ vulgaire"; in alchemy, "vulgar" mercury refers to the common liquid quicksilver found in commerce, which alchemists believed was "dead" and needed to be "quickened" or purified</note>, which cannot nourish nor adhere to metals; for although these metals may be tinted or joined with it, they are easily separated by fire. But if common mercury is boiled philosophically <note>Refers to "Philosophical" boiling, which is a slow, regulated heating in a sealed vessel, distinct from common kitchen boiling</note> with the perfect metals <note>Gold and silver were called "perfect" because they resist corruption by fire or rust</note> in a suitable form and manner, this common mercury draws from them what is most proper, and incorporates it so unanimously with itself that it is impossible to make the separation, any more than one can separate the water boiled with the meats mentioned above. This union of <margin>Read: unclear or missing</margin> common mercury with the perfect metals is of such a nature that, being circulated <note>Circulation is a process of continuous distillation in a closed vessel where the vapor condenses and flows back into the base liquid</note> by the order described by the Philosophers, it is reduced to such a balm, and of...

### Athenagoras, Athenian Philosopher, Book 9, folio 346. On Perfect Love; and he calls this a fable. <note>This likely refers to "Le Vrai et Parfait Amour" (The True and Perfect Love), an alchemical romance published in the 16th century, long after the historical Athenagoras lived.</note>

In less than an instant, this Demon approached Apollo; I know not what illusion blurred my eyes, for I lost sight of this God, and the Demon seemed to remain alone. Then came Vulcan <note>The Roman god of fire and the forge, representing the application of intense heat in the alchemical furnace.</note> wanting, so it seemed to me, to avenge Apollo, using great violence <note>Referring to the intensity of the flame.</note> against this Demon, who being unable to withstand this effort, I was astonished that I saw him no more, and Apollo appeared as before. Now, at the sixth time, both Vulcan and the Demon having departed, Apollo appeared very weak, although his complexion was not diminished, and he complained greatly of having been corrupted by this Demon. The Demon, not content with having tormented him so much, returned once more, bringing with him a companion of his, represented in a white garment which covered him entirely <note>The "white garment" likely refers to the "Albedo" or white stage of the alchemical Great Work, or a specific white precipitate.</note>: These two together invaded Apollo and reduced him to such extremity that he appeared no longer, and this last Demon, stronger than the other, seemed to appear alone. <meta>This page transitions from the end of the alphabetical index to the legal front matter of the book. It contains the "Privilege du Roy," the royal license granting exclusive publishing rights.</meta>

### Royal Privilege.

Louis, by the grace of God King of France & of Navarre, To our beloved and faithful Councilors holding our Courts of Parliaments, Masters of Requests in ordinary of our Household, Bailiffs, Seneschals, Provosts, their Lieutenants, and our other Justices and Officers whom it may concern, Greetings: Our dear and well-beloved Melchior Mondiere <note>A prominent 17th-century Parisian bookseller and printer located on the Rue Saint-Jacques, known for publishing scientific and esoteric works.</note>, Merchant Bookseller and Printer of our City of Paris, has caused to be told and shown to Us that he has recovered a Book entitled *Mystical Harmony or the agreement of the Chemical Philosophers with the Scholia on the most difficult passages of the Authors cited therein, etc.* <note>The "Chemical Philosophers" refers to alchemists, and "Scholia" are explanatory comments or annotations on a primary text.</note> composed by **David Laigneau** <term>Physician-in-ordinary</term> <note>original: "Medecin ordinaire." This title indicates that Laigneau was part of the King's regular medical staff, a position of significant professional status.</note>, which he desires to print or have printed, sell, and distribute; for this purpose, he has petitioned us to grant him our required and necessary letters, desiring to treat him favorably: **FOR THESE CAUSES**, we have permitted and do permit him by these presents to print or have printed, sell or have sold during the term of twelve years the said Book, counting from the day that it shall be finished being printed for the first time, in such form, volume, and characters as shall seem good to him, during which time we make very express inhibitions and prohibitions to all persons from printing it, having it printed, selling, or distributing it under <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "sous" (under) indicates the beginning of the legal penalties for copyright infringement on the following page.</note>

<summary>This page presents the official Royal Privilege granted by King Louis XIII to the printer Melchior Mondière for David Laigneau's alchemical treatise. It establishes a twelve-year monopoly on the printing and sale of the work.</summary>
<keywords>Royal Privilege, Louis XIII, Melchior Mondière, David Laigneau, Alchemy, Book Trade, 17th-century law, Paris</keywords>

--- Page 578 ---
<meta>This page continues the text of the "Privilege du Roy" (Royal Privilege), which began on the previous page. It details the penalties for copyright infringement and the requirements for legal deposit before concluding with the official signatures and the date of printing.</meta>

under any disguise or pretext whatsoever without the consent of the said petitioner, upon penalty of a fine <note>original: "amande," an archaic spelling of "amende."</note> of three thousand livres, half to us, the other half to the [aggrieved] party, the confiscation of the said books, and all costs, damages, and interests, provided that the said petitioner shall be bound to deposit three copies—namely, two in our Library <note>The *Bibliothèque du Roi*, the precursor to the National Library of France.</note> and the third for our very dear and faithful Lord **Séguier** <note>Pierre Séguier (1588–1672) was the Chancellor of France and a powerful patron of the arts and sciences who oversaw the regulation of the book trade.</note>, Knight and Chancellor of France—before offering it for sale, upon penalty of forfeiting the Privilege. **THUS WE COMMAND YOU** that you cause the said petitioner, and those acting on his behalf, to enjoy and use the contents of these presents, without allowing him to be disturbed therein; and by placing at the beginning or at the end of the said Book these presents or a brief extract thereof, we will that they be held as verified. For such is our pleasure. Given at Paris the 1st day of September in the year of grace 1636, and of our reign the twenty-seventh.

->By the King in his Council,<-

->Signed **RENOUARD**.<-

---

->*Printing completed for the first time on September 12, 1636.*<-

<summary>This page concludes the legal publishing license for the book, specifying a 3,000-livre fine for unauthorized copies and the requirement to deposit copies in the royal library. It is signed by a representative of King Louis XIII's council and notes the final printing date.</summary>

<keywords>Royal Privilege, copyright law, fine, Pierre Séguier, legal deposit, Paris, 1636, printing history</keywords>
